# Work Design and Compensation Strategies in a Post-COVID World: Evidence from Online Job Postings

Chunmian Ge¹, Huiqi Deng¹, Dongyuan Wu²*, Hanwei Huang³,⁴,⁵*, James H. Dulebohn⁶

1 School of Business Administration, South China University of Technology, Guangzhou, China

2 School of Management, Fudan University, Shanghai, China

3 Department of Economics and Finance, City University of Hong Kong

4 Centre for Economic Performance, London School of Economics, London, UK

5 Center for International Finance and Economics Research, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China

6 School of Human Resources and Labor Relations, Michigan State University, East Lansing, Michigan, USA

*Correspond to wdy@fudan.edu.cn, huang.hanwei@cityu.edu.hk

This study examines how the COVID-19 pandemic, as a disruptive external shock, has reshaped organizational practices, with particular emphasis on changes in work design and compensation strategies. Drawing on a unique dataset of over 1.8 million online job postings from Chinese publicly listed firms, we employed a large language model to systematically quantify work design characteristics. Using a difference-in-differences approach, we found that the pandemic significantly enhanced skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and work-from-home feasibility. Notably, these effects varied by occupational nonroutineness: occupations with high nonroutineness exhibited larger increases in task significance, but smaller increases in other work design characteristics. Moreover, work design characteristics mediated the relationship between the pandemic and salaries, though these indirect effects were attenuated for nonroutine occupations. Additionally, we observed post-pandemic adjustments in firms' compensation, including notable declines in performance-related pay and insurance benefits, accompanied by increases in subsidies. These findings provide new insights into how organizations redesign work and adjust compensation strategies in response to large-scale external shocks.

Keywords: Work design, Compensation strategies, Work from home, Nonroutineness, COVID-19 pandemic, Online job postings, Large language models

## Introduction

The COVID-19 pandemic, as a large-scale, disruptive event, triggered significant economic, societal, and mortality shocks that brought about profound changes in the global workforce (Chong et al., 2020; Minbaeva & Navrbjerg, 2023). Organizations across industries confronted unprecedented challenges, including disruptions of routine workflows, reconfigurations of roles and responsibilities, and sudden shifts to work-from-home (WFH) arrangements. These changes have not only reshaped work design, affecting both organizational outcomes and individual performance (Humphrey et al., 2007; Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017), but have also prompted organizations to reconsider how redesigned work should be valued and rewarded. This reassessment has, in turn, driven shifts in compensation strategies under financial constraints and heightened uncertainty (DeVaro, 2010; Foss, 2021). Therefore, the COVID-19 pandemic presents a unique opportunity to reevaluate how disruptive external shocks influence work design and compensation strategies, calling into question the applicability of longstanding management theories in times of crisis.

Despite widespread recognition of the transformative effects of disruptive external events on the world of work, little empirical research has systematically examined how these events, notably the COVID-19 pandemic, reshape work design and compensation strategies. Classic work design theories, including the seminal Job Characteristic Model (Hackman & Oldham, 1976) and its extensions (Humphrey et al., 2007), were typically grounded in the assumption of stability in the work environment—an assumption directly challenged by the pandemic. Yet, from the

perspective of event system theory (Morgeson et al., 2015), COVID-19 represents a novel, disruptive, and critical external shock that reconfigured the structure and design of work, altering the skill utilization, feedback mechanisms, coordination patterns, and organization of WFH-feasible roles (Min et al., 2021; Wang et al., 2021). As Hoff (2021) notes, the pandemic provides a compelling context to revisit work design, particularly for occupations involving nonroutine tasks, as it undermined traditional assumptions about professional autonomy and blurred the boundary between work and nonwork. Moreover, as firms were compelled to restructure job roles and adapt to new physical or virtual work environments, questions emerged about how these evolving work design characteristics translated into changes in compensation strategies in response to the pandemic. Recent studies have documented diverse compensation adjustments during the pandemic, ranging from pay cuts (Bahmani et al., 2023) and delayed payments under financial pressure (Zhou et al., 2024) to compensation increases aimed at securing top talent and mitigating turnover in a volatile labor market (e.g., Feldmann, 2022; Sammer, 2021). Understanding this linkage is crucial, as it reveals how large-scale external shocks cascade through organizational systems, recalibrating both work design and compensation strategies to sustain employee performance and ensure organizational continuity.

To empirically examine these relationships, we integrate event system theory and work design theory to investigate how the COVID-19 pandemic reshaped work design characteristics and, in turn, influenced compensation strategies, using a generative large language model (LLM) to analyze over 1.8 million job postings from Chinese publicly listed firms. Event system theory

introduces a dynamic perspective, highlighting that events characterized by high novelty, disruption, and criticality, such as COVID-19, can have profound impacts on job structures and organizational systems (Min et al., 2021; Morgeson et al., 2015; Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017). Complementarily, the Job Characteristics Model (Hackman & Oldham, 1975, 1976) provides a foundational framework for understanding how various dimensions of work design influence employee motivation and performance (Grant & Parker, 2009; Parker & Knight, 2024), thereby shaping employee compensation (Williams et al., 2006). Building on these perspectives, we conceptualize job-level work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, autonomy, task significance, feedback, and WFH feasibility, $ ^{1} $ as mediating mechanisms through which the macro-level shock of COVID-19 is transmitted into changes in compensation strategies. Importantly, the magnitude of these shifts may depend on the nature of occupations (Astakhova et al., 2024). To capture this heterogeneity, we further examine the occupational nonroutineness, defined as the extent to which occupational tasks rely on complex problem-solving, intuition, persuasion, and creativity, as a critical boundary condition (Acemoglu & Autor, 2011; Chauvin et al., 2024) that shapes the pandemic’s impact on work redesign. Our theoretical framework is presented in Figure 1 below.

Integrating these arguments, our study makes four contributions to the literature. First, we advance event system theory and work design theory by empirically demonstrating how disruptive external shocks such as COVID-19 reshape work design characteristics. This deepens our understanding of the evolving nature of work landscape and the dynamics of work design in the post-pandemic era (Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017). To the best of our knowledge, this study is among the first to provide novel, large-scale empirical evidence on how external shocks systematically reshape job-level work design characteristics and compensation. Second, in response to calls for explicit attention to occupational contexts (Dierdorff et al., 2025; Dierdorff & Morgeson, 2013), we enrich work design research by examining the moderating role of occupational nonroutineness (Astakhova et al., 2024; Morgeson et al., 2010). By doing so, we offer a multilevel perspective on how macro-level external shocks interact with occupational attributes, leading to heterogeneous effects on micro-level job designs. Our study also adds to research on occupational resilience by showing how occupational adaptability and organizational adjustments

jointly help sustain core functions and performance in response to external shocks, with effects varying by occupational nonroutineness (Kossek & Perrigino, 2016). Third, we extend the compensation literature by revealing how pandemic-induced changes in work design translate into compensation adjustments and by uncovering the nuanced trends across various forms of compensation, such as merit pay, stock options, training opportunities, subsidies, and insurance, during periods of crisis (Dulebohn et al., 2009; Dulebohn & Werling, 2007). Finally, we contribute methodologically by leveraging LLMs to analyze job-related characteristics from millions of online job postings across time, industries, and regions. This approach demonstrates the potential of LLM-based analyses in Human Resource Management (HRM) research, offering a scalable alternative to traditional methods (Carlson & Burbano, 2025; De Kok, 2025).

#### Literature Review and Hypothesis Development

#### COVID-19 and Work Design

Work design refers to “the content and organization of work tasks, activities, relationships, and responsibilities” (Parker, 2014, p.662). Researchers have long sought to understand how work design can motivate employees and enhance organizational outcomes (Humphrey et al., 2007; Parker & Knight, 2024). Among foundational frameworks, the Job Characteristics Model stands out as one of the most influential frameworks, identifying core job characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, and feedback, that lead to desirable work outcomes by fostering critical psychological states such as meaningfulness and responsibility (Hackman & Oldham, 1975, 1976). To understand how work design evolves in response to

disruptive external shocks (Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017), we draw on event system theory (Morgeson et al., 2015), which conceptualizes external shocks as events that disrupt established routines and necessitate adaptive responses. The COVID-19 pandemic represents such a disruptive external shock that triggered both top-down and cross-level effects on organizations and individuals (McFarland et al., 2020; Min et al., 2021). In response, organizations were compelled to reconfigure the fundamental design of work as a key solution for adaptation.

Several studies have explored the pandemic-induced shifts in employees' work experiences and the broader design of organizational work structures. For example, Dhir et al. (2024) examined how the pandemic reshaped work dynamics and found a heightened sense of social isolation and exclusion from the work environment. Their interview-based study identified key practices for promoting employee inclusion, such as clearly defined roles, flexible work arrangements, and work-life balance. Similarly, Chong et al. (2020) found that daily COVID-19 task setbacks were associated with end-of-day emotional exhaustion, which led to next-day work withdrawal. This effect was exacerbated by high task interdependence but alleviated by telework task support. Additional evidence indicates that many organizations, either voluntarily or out of necessity, incorporated WFH opportunities into their job postings during the pandemic, marking one of the most profound and enduring shifts in the organization of work (Barrero et al., 2021; Ham et al., 2024). This pandemic-induced shift reflects employers' growing recognition and accelerated reconfiguration of jobs for remote work. Consequently, it positions WFH feasibility as a distinct work design characteristic that defines how tasks, activities, relationships, and responsibilities are

structured and organized across spatial and technological boundaries (Dingel & Neiman, 2020; Parker, 2014; Wang et al., 2021). Collectively, these findings underscore the pandemic's role as both a destabilizing and transformational event that redefined work design priorities (Collings, McMackin, et al., 2021; Minbaeva & Navrbjerg, 2023).

Drawing on the Job Characteristic Model, we examine how the COVID-19 pandemic systematically influenced core work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, and feedback. Moreover, we introduce WFH feasibility as an additional work design characteristic, reflecting the potential flexibility of jobs performed remotely and organizations' adaptive work redesign in response to the evolving challenges posed by the pandemic. The first dimension, skill variety, refers to the degree to which a job requires an individual to use a range of skills and abilities to complete different tasks (Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006). During the pandemic, organizations experienced acute operational disruptions due to employee absence caused by illness, quarantine, or mobility restrictions. To sustain operations, many firms expanded job roles and promoted multiskilling, enabling employees to flexibly cover for absent colleagues and ensure workflow continuity (Collings, Nyberg, et al., 2021). Disrupted routines and reduced informal collaboration further necessitated cross-functional work, compelling employees to acquire new skills while continuing to perform their routine tasks (Leroy et al., 2021). Similar adjustments also occurred in routine service sectors such as retail and hospitality, where employees assumed broader tasks, including managing hygiene protocols, coordinating online and offline services, and supporting operational redesign and digitalization to

sustain service delivery. Continuous adaptation to pandemic-induced disruptions, including workflow reconfiguration and experimentation with new practices, also amplified the demand for diverse skills to manage emerging challenges (Chong et al., 2020).

Second, task identity refers to the extent to which a job involves completing a whole and identifiable piece of work (Hackman & Oldham, 1976). The pandemic heightened the salience of task identity as organizations sought to preserve workflow continuity under disrupted routines and weakened informal coordination. These disruptions complicated the execution of interdependent tasks that often involved direct and face-to-face interactions (Foss, 2021). In response, organizations reassigned employees greater ownership over business processes to reduce reciprocal dependencies and ensure task completion even with limited staffing (Chong et al., 2020; Foss, 2021). Moreover, across on-site, hybrid, and remote work settings, task identity has emerged as a critical work design characteristic that fosters employees' sense of purpose and accountability, sustains their energy, and prompts continued engagement (Lamovšek et al., 2025). Supporting this, Chong et al. (2020) found that high task interdependence in telework settings amplified the strain of daily setbacks, leading to emotional exhaustion and work withdrawal. To mitigate these effects, organizations sought to reduce process fragmentation and enhance task identity (Lamovšek et al., 2025; Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017).

Third, task significance refers to the degree to which a job has a substantial impact on other people (Hackman & Oldham, 1976). During the pandemic, many employees experienced heightened task significance, as the societal relevance of their work became more visible. For

example, essential workers (e.g., Yuan et al., 2021), including nonphysician healthcare workers (Hennekam et al., 2020) and gig workers (Cameron et al., 2022), found that their tasks, once regarded as routine, now directly contributed to public health and community well-being. Their roles were morally elevated in public discourse, portrayed as “heroes” working on the frontlines (Hennekam et al., 2020). At the same time, employees in non-essential but functionally critical sectors, such as business and finance, information technology, and education, increasingly recognized how their efforts sustained organizational operations and protected others’ livelihoods (Lips-Wiersma et al., 2016). Reflecting this shift, Minbaeva and Navrbjerg (2023) emphasized that a key strategic HRM practice during the pandemic was to highlight the importance of employees to the organizations, regardless of whether they were on-site, remote, or furloughed.

Fourth, autonomy refers to the degree of freedom, independence, and discretion employees have over how, where, and when they complete their work tasks (Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006). The pandemic-induced shifts to remote or hybrid work arrangements enhanced employees' sense of control over their work schedules, locations, and methods (Becker et al., 2022; McAlpine et al., 2024). Reduced face-to-face supervision and the increased need for decentralized decision-making promoted organizations to delegate authority to line managers and empower individuals to manage daily tasks (Foss, 2021; Minbaeva & Navrbjerg, 2023). This shift reflected a structural increase in job autonomy, a core motivational characteristic that fosters self-efficacy, personal initiative, and proactive work behavior (Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017; Wang et al., 2021). Moreover, heightened uncertainty intensified the demand for adaptive, nonroutine decision-making (Cordery et al., 2010;

Yoon et al., 2021), making autonomy essential for employees to flexibly address emerging challenges and sustain performance. Autonomy also became increasingly valued in frontline roles. For example, McKinsey & Company reported that autonomy (i.e., predictable schedules, discretion, and control over task execution) emerged as a key motivator for retail workers (Fuller et al., 2022). Similarly, Becker et al. (2022) noted that as individuals experienced reduced control in personal and social domains, autonomy at work provided a crucial source of psychological control and stability.

Fifth, feedback refers to the extent to which employees receive direct and clear information about their performance, either from the job itself or from others within the organization (Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006). Although the COVID-19 pandemic significantly limited opportunities for immediate, in-person feedback due to reduced face-to-face interactions (Chauvin et al., 2024), the disruptions to traditional work arrangements and rapid shifts toward digital collaboration promoted the development of new feedback mechanisms (Muzio & Doh, 2021). As teams became physically dispersed, organizations increasingly relied on digital tools, such as computer monitoring, online meetings, and virtual check-ins, to enable real-time communication and progress tracking (Delfino & Van Der Kolk, 2021; Ko & Baek, 2024; Laker et al., 2020). For example, digital technologies automatically record employees' activities and generate detailed logs, facilitating targeted, timely feedback and improving work efficiency (Leonardi, 2021). These systems enable more frequent, measurable, and transparent performance evaluations. Moreover, as post-pandemic work became more autonomous and self-directed, feedback processes evolved

from supervisor-led evaluations to peer-based feedback, fostering shared learning, open communication, and decentralized collaboration (Minbaeva & Navrbjerg, 2023; Reiche, 2023).

Lastly, WFH feasibility refers to the extent to which the work activities can be performed at home (Dingel & Neiman, 2020). Although WFH had existed for decades before the pandemic, it was often perceived as signaling reduced commitment or visibility to employers and coworkers (McAlpine et al., 2024). However, COVID-19 prompted organizations to rapidly expand WFH policies, alleviating the stigma associated with such practices and accelerating the recognition of roles that could be feasibly performed outside the workplace (Becker et al., 2022; Bolino et al., 2024). To maintain business continuity during lockdowns, firms restructured workflows and reallocated tasks to enhance location flexibility, thereby broadening the scope of WFH-feasible roles (Chong et al., 2020). Ham et al. (2024) also found that firms disclosed more WFH-feasible job postings to attract talent and adapt to intensified labor market competition during the pandemic. This shift was further supported by the increasing use of digital tools and growing managerial experience in coordinating distributed teams. While knowledge-intensive roles in sectors such as IT, finance, and design readily adapted to remote work, occupations requiring physical presence, specialized equipment, or high interpersonal interaction remain constrained in WFH feasibility (Dingel & Neiman, 2020). Therefore, although not all jobs were feasible for WFH, the pandemic overall expanded organizational recognition of its potential, prompting firms to redesign roles, adopt digital infrastructures, and formalize flexible work policies, thereby integrating WFH feasibility more prominently into contemporary work design.

Taken together, we argue that COVID-19 compelled organizations to redesign jobs by enhancing their motivating potential as an adaptive response to heightened uncertainty. In doing so, organizations sought to strengthen core work design characteristics that facilitate employee engagement, adaptability, and performance under crisis conditions, while also integrating WFH feasibility into work design to accommodate social distancing challenges. Consequently, we expect the pandemic to increase skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. Therefore, we propose the following hypothesis:

Hypothesis 1 (H1): The COVID-19 pandemic increased work design characteristics of (a) skill variety, (b) task identity, (c) task significance, (d) autonomy, (e) feedback, and (f) WFH feasibility.

#### Moderating Role of Occupational Nonroutineness

Occupations differ substantially in their cognitive demands, task complexity, resource

access, and coordination mechanisms. Identified as a particularly salient form of context, an

occupation refers to a group of work roles across organizations that share common work

requirements (e.g., tasks, responsibilities), work methods, goals, or worker qualifications (e.g.,

knowledge, skills, abilities) (Morgeson et al., 2010). Compared with specific job characteristics,

occupations operate as higher-level contextual structures that exert top-down influences on job

design (Dierdorff & Morgeson, 2013), defining the distribution of tasks, skills requirements, and

role interdependence, all of which are central elements of work design (Parker, Van Den Broeck,

et al., 2017). Because occupations inherently define and constrain work design characteristics,

these contexts play a pivotal role in shaping the extent and direction of how organizations redesign work in response to disruptive external shocks (Morgeson et al., 2010; Parker, 2014).

Within the broader occupational contexts, nonroutineness represents a key dimension that captures fundamental differences in task structure across occupations, as recognized in the labor economics literature (Cortes et al., 2017; Reijnders & De Vries, 2018). Acemoglu and Autor (2011) argue that all occupations comprise both routine and nonroutine tasks but vary in the extent to which each type predominates. For example, professional, managerial, and technical occupations typically specialize in nonroutine cognitive tasks, such as analytical reasoning, complex problem-solving, intuition, and creativity, whereas clerical, production, and service roles, distinguished as routine occupations, rely more on standardized, repetitive procedures. Nonroutine occupations are thus typically characterized by high autonomy, flexibility, intellectual engagement, and cross-functional coordination (Adler & Kwon, 2013; Toppinen-Tanner et al., 2002), constituting the intellectual and operational core of modern organizations (Scott, 2008). In this sense, nonroutineness functions as a critical occupational context that can either facilitate or constrain the development of various work design characteristics (Astakhova et al., 2024; Dierdorff & Morgeson, 2013). Consequently, when external shocks such as COVID-19 disrupt established work systems, occupational nonroutineness becomes salient (Hoff, 2021), as it captures broad task differences across occupations while also serving as an essential attribute shaping the magnitude and direction of work redesign effects.

We propose that occupational nonroutineness moderates the relationship between COVID-

19 and work design characteristics. First, routine occupations, characterized by standardized tasks and limited control over work processes (Birtch et al., 2024), tend to be more amenable to restructuring through task redistributions and cross-training, thereby enhancing skill variety and task identity in response to disruptive external shocks. By contrast, nonroutine occupations (i.e., physicians, data scientists) demand advanced cognitive judgment, numerous decision-making, and tacit domain expertise to address unexpected and complex problems (Astakhova et al., 2024; Chauvin et al., 2024). These characteristics indicate that nonroutine occupations already exhibit high levels of problem-solving capability and responsibility, making them less susceptible to the need for substantial skill expansion or task reconfiguration during crises (Kossek & Perrigino, 2016). However, regarding task significance, the pandemic heightened the experience of meaningful work more strongly for nonroutine occupations, as their work is often connected with critical organizational operations and societal contributions that became more salient during the crisis (Lips-Wiersma et al., 2016). In contrast, routine occupations, such as administrative assistants and assembly-line workers, were generally less directly linked to organizational or societal outcomes, resulting in comparatively modest increases in task significance.

Furthermore, the pandemic-induced disruptions to face-to-face supervision and the shifts to remote work generally increased employees' autonomy (Becker et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2021). Yet, this effect was less pronounced for nonroutine occupations, where employees already operated with substantial discretion and self-direction in their work (Adler & Kwon, 2013; Väänänen et al., 2008). Moreover, many organizations introduced or reinforced formal coordination mechanisms,

such as standardized reporting and cross-functional reviews, to mitigate risk and ensure accountability, thereby limiting further increases in autonomy for these roles. Similarly, the positive effect of the pandemic on feedback was weaker for nonroutine occupations, where rich interpersonal exchanges and interpretive discussions were difficult to replicate through structured digital tools (Chauvin et al., 2024; Minbaeva & Navrbjerg, 2023). In contrast, routine occupations adapted more readily to instrumented feedback mechanisms, such as transaction counts and turnaround times, which translated effectively into virtual formats and facilitated sustainable, measurable performance monitoring.

Finally, the increase in WFH feasibility during the pandemic was more pronounced in routine than nonroutine occupations. While employees in nonroutine occupations are typically highly knowledgeable and technologically capable of remote work, their tasks often rely on tacit knowledge sharing, collaborative problem-solving, and access to specialized or secure facilities such as laboratories, clinical sites, or restricted data systems (Astakhova et al., 2024; Chauvin et al., 2024). These interdependencies constrain the potential for further remote execution in nonroutine roles. By contrast, routine occupations, such as clerical and accounting staff, involve standardized, codifiable tasks that are more easily digitalized and relocated to remote settings.

Taken together, nonroutine occupations are cognitively demanding and inherently enriched in their work design (Astakhova et al., 2024; Toppinen-Tanner et al., 2002), leaving limited scope for organizations to further enhance their work design characteristics such as skill variety, task identity, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. However, employees in these occupations

experienced a larger increase in task significance, as they were better able to recognize how their work contributed to broader organizational goals or societal implications (Lips-Wiersma et al., 2016). Therefore, we propose the following hypothesis:

Hypothesis 2 (H2): Occupational nonroutineness moderates the positive impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on work design characteristics, such that the effects are weaker for occupations with high nonroutineness than for those with low nonroutineness for (a) skill variety, (b) task identity, (c) autonomy, (d) feedback, and (e) WFH feasibility, but stronger for (f) task significance.

#### Work Design and Compensation

Economics-based organizational design theory suggests that changes in work design are often accompanied by adjustments in compensation strategies (Jensen & Meckling, 2009). Williams et al. (2006) argued that work design is an essential determinant of pay: Individuals working in enriched jobs, characterized by higher skill requirements and greater responsibility for task completion, typically earn more than those in less enriched roles (Parker, 2014). Scholars have also proposed that both motivational work design and compensation serve as rewards that improve employee experiences and productivity (e.g., Campion & Berger, 1990; DeVaro, 2010). Supporting this view, Campion and Berger (1990) found a positive relationship between motivational work design characteristics and compensation in an analysis of over 100 job types. Despite these insights, scholars continue to call for research on the relationship between work design and compensation, as existing empirical evidence remains limited and provides only preliminary support for a positive

relationship (e.g., Campion & Berger, 1990; DeVaro, 2010).

We propose that work design characteristics are positively associated with compensation (Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006). Specifically, regarding skill variety, human capital theory suggests that jobs requiring greater skill complexity and personal investments tend to command higher compensation, as pay serves as a mechanism for attracting qualified candidates and enabling selective recruitment (Jin et al., 2010; Milkovich et al., 2014). When designing roles with increased skill demands, organizations must align compensation with external labor market benchmarks (Ehrenberg et al., 2021). Our view that compensation increases with enriched work design characteristics is consistent with existing empirical evidence (Parker, 2014). For example, Duan et al. (2024), using Chinese online job posting data, found that higher skill variety is associated with wage premiums, reflecting the value of broader skill sets.

Second, for task identity, jobs that allow employees to complete an entire, identifiable piece of work typically entail greater ownership, accountability, and intrinsic motivation. This heightened sense of responsibility often justifies higher compensation, as these roles demand broader knowledge, skills, and abilities, and greater commitment (Humphrey et al., 2007; Parker, 2014). Empirical research by Kinicki et al. (2002) supports this, showing a positive correlation between task identity and pay.

Third, task significance, defined as the perceived impact of one's work on others, has been shown to enhance job performance through increased dedication (Grant & Parker, 2009). Organizations may offer higher compensation to employees whose work generates substantial

internal or external value, as a means of recognition and retention (Rynes et al., 2005).

Fourth, regarding autonomy, Fang and Tilcsik (2022) found that work autonomy helps explain part of the class-based pay gap, as individuals with upper-class parents are more likely to enter highly autonomous, well-compensated occupations such as lawyers, oral surgeons, and securities traders. From an efficiency wage perspective, firms may also offer higher pay for autonomous roles to compensate for reduced monitoring, as increased wages help ensure worker effort in an environment where direct oversight is limited (Arai, 1994).

Fifth, feedback, whether formal or informal, is critical for clarifying role expectations and reinforcing desired behaviors. Constructive feedback not only helps employees understand what is valued by the organization, thereby sustaining their motivation (Tolli & Schmidt, 2008), but also enables managers to monitor progress toward performance goals. Research indicates that both feedback and compensation are critical levers for motivating employee performance (Christ et al., 2016). Therefore, jobs designed with more frequent, actionable feedback are associated with higher pay to sustain engagement and drive performance.

Finally, occupations with higher WFH feasibility were better positioned to maintain work continuity and productivity during the pandemic, allowing organizations to adapt more effectively to social distancing requirements. Gajendran and Harrison (2007) found that such roles typically offer greater flexibility and autonomy in how and where employees perform work, thereby enhancing job satisfaction and performance. These factors, in turn, are associated with higher compensation (Williams et al., 2006). Moreover, WFH feasibility often signals greater job

complexity and higher reliance on digital tools, both of which are positively valued in compensation strategies. Empirical evidence supports this view, showing that the post-pandemic shift towards WFH feasibility is associated with an increase in income (Bonacini et al., 2021).

Overall, given that COVID-19 has a profound impact on work design, and motivational work design positively influences compensation, we propose the following hypotheses:

Hypothesis 3 (H3): The work design characteristics of (a) skill variety, (b) task identity, (c) task significance, (d) autonomy, (e) feedback, and (f) WFH feasibility mediate the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on job salaries.

Building on these hypotheses, we further propose a moderated mediation hypothesis. Figure 1 presents the proposed model.

Hypothesis 4 (H4): Occupational nonroutineness moderates the indirect effect of the COVID-19 pandemic on job salaries through enhanced work design characteristics, namely (a) skill variety, (b) task identity, (c) task significance, (d) autonomy, (e) feedback, and (f) WFH feasibility.

#### COVID-19 and Total Compensation

Building on our analysis of how the COVID-19 pandemic influences job salaries through work design, we further examine its effects on the composition of total compensation. Total compensation encompasses all forms of financial returns and tangible services and benefits that employees receive from their employment, including cash compensation such as wages and merit pay, and benefits such as allowances and subsidies (Milkovich et al., 2014). Drawing on the event

system theory, large-scale disruptions such as COVID-19 not only alter work structures but also prompt organizations to reevaluate compensation strategies to sustain motivation, retention, and well-being amid changing job demands and work arrangements. For example, organizations facing financial uncertainties may reduce or suspend merit pay while expanding benefits, such as subsidies to support employee well-being (e.g., SHRM, 2021). Yet, empirical evidence remains limited on how the pandemic has differentially influenced distinct components of total compensation and whether these adjustments vary across occupations with different levels of nonroutineness. To address this gap, we explore the following research question:

Research Question: How does COVID-19 differentially affect total compensation, such as merit pay, stock options, training opportunities, subsidies, and insurance, and to what extent are these effects moderated by the occupational nonroutineness?

## Method

### Data Sample

We used a unique dataset of 1,836,062 job postings from 3,183 publicly listed firms from 2018 to 2022, sourced from one of China's leading online job posting platforms. Similar datasets have been widely used in management research to explore various labor market issues (Ge et al., 2023; Ham et al., 2024). The dataset provides detailed daily information on job postings, making it suitable for analyzing firms' work design and compensation strategies over time.

Each job posting contains granular information about the position and employer, including the job title, job description, expected salary, required education and work experience, and benefits.

The job descriptions provide details about requirements and responsibilities, enabling us to assess job characteristics using LLMs. To consider occupational heterogeneity, we mapped each job posting to the Dictionary of Occupational Titles (DOT) and the Occupational Information Network (O*NET) database based on the translated job title and functional keywords following Ge et al. (2023). Additionally, we utilized employer-level information, such as company names and industries, to match firms in the job posting data with external data sources for further analyses (see Appendix A for details about data collection).

#### LLM-based Measures

Recent advances in generative LLMs, have demonstrated their potential to replicate human-like responses and assessments in social research (Banker et al., 2024; De Kok, 2025), achieving high consistency with human annotations in tasks such as occupational analysis and psychological experiment replication (Carlson & Burbano, 2025; Chen et al., 2025). This approach enables the analysis of large volumes of unstructured data in a short time, offering significant methodological advantages over traditional survey-based methods in terms of scalability and cost-effectiveness (De Kok, 2025). Unlike surveys that may reflect individuals' idiosyncratic reactions to jobs, LLMs evaluate the job itself, minimizing interpretation bias while ensuring greater objectivity and consistency (Chen et al., 2024; Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006).

In this study, we used an advanced LLM, OpenAI's GPT-4o-mini, to score the six work design characteristics. Specifically, we operationalized this process through an automated LLM-based scoring framework following De Kok (2025) and Carlson and Burbano (2025). First, we

developed a prompt to instruct the LLM to perform assessment tasks based on job titles and descriptions. A complete prompt is presented in Appendix B. It incorporated domain knowledge and contextual information from work design and remote work research, including definitions of the six work design characteristics (Dingel & Neiman, 2020; Hackman & Oldham, 1975; Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006). Second, we assigned GPT-4o-mini the role of a professional evaluator in the field of HRM to assess work design characteristics. The prompt design applied techniques such as role prompting (i.e., “You are a professional evaluator of work design characteristics in the field of human resource management.”) and few-shot prompting, $ ^{2} $ in which a sample assessment was incorporated to guide the model’s in-context learning (Carlson & Burbano, 2025; De Kok, 2025). Third, we included an instruction to output results in a structured list format ([id, skill variety score, task identity score, task significance score, autonomy score, feedback score, WFH]). Additionally, we set the temperature hyperparameter to 0 to reduce variability and ensure consistency across model runs (Carlson & Burbano, 2025). $ ^{3} $ As a result, GPT-4o-mini successfully generated valid scores for the five work design characteristics on a 5-point Likert scale (i.e., 1 = very low to 5 = very high) (Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006) and for WFH feasibility on a binary scale (i.e., 0 = infeasible or 1 = feasible) (Dingel & Neiman, 2020).

#### Measures

Work design characteristics. Based on the LLM-based approach above, we developed measures for the six work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility, derived from online job postings. The former five core characteristics were measured on a 5-point Likert scale, ranging from 1 (Very Low) to 5 (Very High). Furthermore, WFH was measured as a binary variable, assigned a value of 1 if a given job posting was classified as feasible for WFH and 0 otherwise.

Salary. Job salary was derived from the salary or salary range specified in each job posting.

Salary was measured as the natural logarithm of the monthly salary (in ten thousand yuan) offered for a certain position. For postings providing a salary range, Salary was calculated as the natural logarithm of the average monthly salary within the specified range.

COVID Case. To assess the impact of COVID-19 on job postings from publicly listed firms, we created a variable, COVID Case, to capture the pandemic’s intensity across Chinese cities. This measure took a value of 0 in the pre-COVID-19 period, and in the post-COVID-19 period, was computed as the cumulative confirmed COVID-19 cases divided by the city population (per thousand people) in 2022 (Min et al., 2021).

Nonroutineness. Following prior research (Acemoglu & Autor, 2011; Autor et al., 2003; Chauvin et al., 2024), we assigned a continuous nonroutineness score to each job posting based on its occupational category, using a task-based measure from the O*NET database. Specifically, we first mapped occupations in our job posting data to their corresponding O*NET occupations (see

Appendix A for details). We then constructed composite indices of nonroutineness using the Work Activities and Work Context Importance scales in the O*NET database. The index comprised six dimensions: (1) analyzing data or information, (2) thinking creatively, (3) interpreting information for others, (4) establishing and maintaining personal relationships, (5) guiding, directing, and motivating subordinates, and (6) coaching and developing others. Each scale was standardized to have a mean of zero and a standard deviation of one based on the O*NET occupations. The composite nonroutineness score was then computed as the sum of the standardized component scales and subsequently mapped to each job posting based on its corresponding O*NET occupation. To ensure the comparability across occupations, we standardized the measure (mean = 0, SD = 1) following Acemoglu and Autor (2011). Table A1 and Figure A1 in Appendix A illustrate the distributions of nonroutineness across occupations, indicating that managerial, professional, and technical occupations are most specialized in abstract, non-routine cognitive tasks.

Control variables. Following prior research (Hsu & Tambe, 2024; Shaw et al., 2002), we included several control variables relevant to our study's focus. First, we accounted for human capital requirements, such as educational attainment and work experience, as both can influence work design characteristics and compensation (Marques et al., 2023; Shaw et al., 2002). To control for educational attainment, we created three dummy variables representing different degree levels: bachelor's degree, master's degree, and doctoral degree. Job postings requiring less than a bachelor's degree (e.g., high school diploma) or not specifying any educational requirement served as the reference group. Work experience was measured as the average number of years required in

each job posting. To capture differences in recruitment and compensation strategies across firms, we incorporated firm-level controls derived from the Chinese Securities Market and Accounting Research Database (CSMAR). Firm size was measured as the natural logarithm of the total number of employees (Katou & Budhwar, 2010; Shaw et al., 2002). Firm age, measured as the natural logarithm of the number of years since founding, was included to capture organizational maturity effects and founding values that may influence compensation (Katou & Budhwar, 2010; Shaw et al., 2002). We also included firms' return on assets (net income divided by total assets) and capital intensity (the natural logarithm of total assets divided by total employment) to control for variations in capital investment and their potential effects on HRM and compensation strategies (Katou & Budhwar, 2010). Moreover, we included city-level GDP per capita (in ten thousand yuan) and unemployment rate $ ^{4} $ from the CSMAR dataset and China City Statistical Yearbooks, capturing local economic development and labor market conditions that may influence work design and compensation. Finally, our specifications included year, industry, city, and job type $ ^{5} $ fixed effects to account for unobserved heterogeneity. All continuous variables were winsorized at the 1st and 99th percentiles to mitigate the influence of outliers. $ ^{6} $

#### Construct Validity

Examining the validity of LLM-based assessments is essential to ensure that this approach is capable of assessing work design characteristics from textual job descriptions (Carlson & Burbano, 2025; De Kok, 2025). We took several steps to confirm that the LLM-generated scores of key constructs demonstrate desirable psychometric properties (McKenny et al., 2018; Sajjadiani et al., 2019). Specifically, we assessed content validity through theory-driven prompt design and targeted manual review of LLM-generated scores, and evaluated convergent validity by comparing the LLM ratings with expert ratings and established measures.

Content Validity. We first ensured content validity by anchoring our prompt design in established theoretical frameworks. Definitions and rating standards for each construct were developed based on a thorough literature review on work design (e.g., Hackman & Oldham, 1975; Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017) and remote work (e.g., Dingel & Neiman, 2020; Min et al., 2021). Members of the author team carefully reviewed and refined the construct definitions to ensure conceptual clarity, theoretical alignment, and practical relevance to ensure content validity (Levashina & Campion, 2007). After the initial prompt design, we compared the definitions, resolved discrepancies through discussion, and reached a consensus on the final rubrics. These construct definitions were embedded into the prompts to ensure that the LLM-generated scores reflected the intended theoretical dimensions with consistency and validity.

To verify that the LLM-generated scores meaningfully distinguished between constructs, we manually reviewed a randomly selected sample of scores. Members of the author team assessed

whether the assigned scores accurately reflected the content of each job description. As shown in Appendix C, Figure C1, we present an example of LLM-generated scoring with brief explanations for each of the six work design characteristics. This review process confirmed that the LLM-generated assessments are conceptually coherent and consistent with the theoretical foundations of the constructs.

Convergent Validity. To assess convergent validity, we examined the convergence between LLM-generated scores and both expert-coded ratings and established benchmark scores. First, we conducted a validation study by comparing the LLM-derived scores with independent human expert ratings (De Kok, 2025). A total of 30 experts with advanced academic backgrounds in management independently rated a random sample of 50 job postings on five core work design characteristics using a 5-point Likert scale and WFH feasibility using a binary scale. The experts were instructed to base their evaluations on job descriptions, construct definitions, and their domain knowledge. The procedures and materials used for expert scoring are detailed in Appendix C. The expert raters demonstrate strong agreement on their ratings, as shown in Appendix C, Table C1. Specifically, the single-rater two-way random intraclass correlations (referred to as ICC(1) by McGraw & Wong, 1996) range from .33 (Feedback) to .57 (Skill Variety), exceeding the acceptable threshold for small (.01), medium (.10), and large (.25) effect sizes (Woehr et al., 2015). The reliability of the aggregated expert scores, ICC(2), is uniformly high, ranging from .94 (Feedback) to .98 (Skill Variety), indicating acceptable reliability of expert ratings across all dimensions. Furthermore, the LLM-generated scores demonstrate strong convergence with expert ratings: for

skill variety, r = .84, p < .001; for task identity, r = .69, p < .001; for task significance, r = .77, p < .001; for autonomy, r = .66, p < .001; for feedback, r = .56, p < .001; and for WFH feasibility, r = .75, p < .001. These results provide robust evidence of the alignment between LLM-based assessments and human expert judgments.

Furthermore, we evaluated the convergent validity of the LLM-generated scores for WFH feasibility by comparing them with the benchmark measure developed by Dingel and Neiman (2020). Specifically, each job posting in our dataset was mapped to this benchmark measure (denoted as WFH_DN) using O*NET occupational classifications. We assessed the proportion of agreement and statistical correlations between the LLM-generated scores and the benchmark measure (see Appendix C for details). We observed a 74.31% agreement between the two measures and strong positive correlations between the continuous proxy measures for WFH capability (r > .60, p < .001). These findings demonstrate a high level of consistency between the LLM-generated scores and the benchmark WFH measure, reinforcing the validity and reliability of the LLM-based approach and its potential to generate human-like responses and assessments from unstructured job descriptions.

#### Analytical Strategy

Our empirical strategy employed a difference-in-differences (DID) approach to compare job postings from publicly listed firms located in cities that experienced varying degrees of

COVID-19 impact on work design characteristics. $ ^{7} $ To capture greater variation in the data, we used a continuous measure of treatment intensity, defined as the ratio of confirmed COVID-19 cases to city population (Ge et al., 2023). Our main specifications included controls for observable job posting characteristics, firm-level characteristics, and regional economic conditions. In addition, we accounted for unobserved time-invariant fixed effects across cities, industries, and job types, as well as time fixed effects. Our empirical specification was as follows:

 $ y_{int} = a_0 + b_1 COVID\ Case_{nt} + \sum_k c_k Controls_{int}^k + Year_t + Industry_n + City_n + Job\ type_i + \varepsilon_{int}(1) $

where i, n, and t index job postings, employing firms, and years, respectively, and  $ y_{int} $ refers to

the six work design characteristics: skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy,

feedback, and WFH feasibility. COVID Case $ _{nt} $ is the number of confirmed cases in the city where

the firm is located. Controls $ _{int}^{k} $ is a set of control variables, including required education and work

experience, firm size, years since founding, capital intensity, city-level GDP per capita, and

unemployment rate.  $ \varepsilon_{int} $ is an error term. We further controlled for year, industry, city, and job

type fixed effects. In this model, the coefficient  $ b_{l} $ of the variable COVID Case $ _{nt} $ captures the

impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on work design characteristics.

To examine the heterogeneous effects of COVID-19 on the six work design characteristics across occupations with varying nonroutineness, we extended the above DID regression:

 $$ \begin{aligned}y_{int}&=a_{0}+b_{1}COVID\ Case_{nt}+b_{2}COVID\ Case_{nt}\times Nonroutineness_{i}+b_{3}Nonroutineness_{i}\\&+\sum_{k}c_{k}Controls_{int}^{k}+Year_{t}+Industry_{n}+City_{n}+Job\ type_{i}+\varepsilon_{int}\\ \end{aligned} $$ 

where Nonroutineness is the moderating variable capturing the occupational nonroutineness of each job posting. Our coefficient of interest,  $ b_{2} $, associated with the interaction term COVID  $ Case_{nt} \times Nonroutineness_{i} $, captures the differential impact of COVID-19 on work design characteristics across occupations with varying nonroutineness. To account for the time-varying impact and the heterogeneous impact across cities with varying severities of COVID-19, particularly across occupations with varying nonroutineness, we included control variables of the interaction terms  $ Post \times Nonroutineness $ and  $ COVID\ Intensity \times Nonroutineness $, respectively. Post is a dummy variable equal to 1 during the COVID-19 period, and COVID Intensity is the cumulative number of confirmed COVID-19 cases divided by the city’s population in 2022. Given that five core work design characteristics were measured on a discrete, ordinal scale ranging from 1 to 5, we employed ordered probit regression to estimate the above equations. As WFH feasibility is a binary variable, we used probit regression to estimate the effect of COVID-19 on WFH feasibility. Table 1 shows the correlations between the variables used in our analysis.

Correlations equal to or above  $ |.00| $ are significant at p < .001 except for the correlation between Task Significance and Unemployment Rate (p < 0.01), between WFH and Firm Age (p < .05), between Doctoral Degree and Unemployment Rate (p < .05), and between COVID Case and Doctoral Degree (p > .1).

## Results

#### COVID-19 and Work Design

Table 2 reports the baseline regression results of equation (1). As shown in column (1), the impact of COVID-19 on skill variety is significant and positive (b = 0.018,  $ \beta = 0.031 $, p < .001), indicating that the pandemic has prompted organizations to design jobs requiring a broader range of skills to adapt to the evolving work conditions and operational challenges. Thus, H1a is supported. Similarly, columns (2) to (6) show significant and positive coefficients of COVID Case on task identity (b = 0.014,  $ \beta = 0.027 $, p < .001), task significance (b = 0.011,  $ \beta = 0.025 $, p < .001), autonomy (b = 0.024,  $ \beta = 0.031 $, p < .001), feedback (b = 0.023,  $ \beta = 0.041 $, p < .001), and WFH feasibility (b = 0.026,  $ \beta = 0.080 $, p < .001), indicating that COVID-19 had a positive impact on work design characteristics in terms of task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. Therefore, H1 is fully supported.

#### Moderating Role of Occupational Nonroutineness

Table 3 presents the results for the moderating effect of occupational nonroutineness on the relationship between COVID-19 and work design characteristics, as specified in equation (2). The coefficients of COVID Case × Nonroutineness capture differential effects of COVID-19 across occupations with varying nonroutineness. The coefficients of the interaction term are significant and negative for skill variety (b = -0.013,  $ \beta = -0.018 $, p < .001) and task identity (b = -0.016,  $ \beta = -0.023 $, p < .001) in columns (1) and (2), suggesting that the positive effects of COVID-19 on both work design characteristics were attenuated for occupations with high

nonroutineness. Conversely, the coefficient of COVID Case  $ \times $ Nonroutineness for task significance  $ (b = 0.007, \beta = 0.009, p < .001) $ in column (3) is significant and positive. This implies that the pandemic’s positive effect on task significance was more pronounced for nonroutine occupations, possibly because their daily tasks are more closely linked to organizational decision-making and

broader social impact. In addition, the interaction term of COVID-19 and nonroutineness on autonomy  $ (b = -0.014, \beta = -0.020, p < .001) $, feedback  $ (b = -0.014, \beta = -0.020, p < .001) $, and WFH feasibility  $ (b = -0.010, \beta = -0.014, p < .001) $ are significant and negative, indicating that the positive effects of COVID-19 on these work design characteristics were weaker for nonroutine occupations than for routine occupations. To summarize, these findings provide empirical support for H2 regarding skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility.

#### Mediating Role of Work Design Characteristics

 $ H_{3} $ suggests that work design characteristics mediate the impact of COVID-19 on job salaries. We examined the indirect effect of COVID-19 on salaries with bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs) to determine the significance of the mediation effects (MacKinnon et al., 2002). As shown in Table 4, the effect of COVID-19 is significant and positive on all six work design characteristics after including controls and fixed effects. Furthermore, column (2) shows the significant coefficients of COVID Case (b = 0.008,  $ \beta = 0.018 $, p < .001) and skill variety (b = 0.169,  $ \beta = 0.242 $, p < .001). According to MacKinnon et al. (2002), the indirect effect via skill variety is calculated as  $ 0.011 \times 0.169 = 0.0018 $. The 95% bootstrap CI of the indirect effect excludes 0 [0.0016, 0.0021]. Thus, the indirect effect is significant, and skill variety partially mediates the relationship between COVID-19 and job salaries, supporting H3a. Similarly, task identity (indirect effect = 0.0004, 95% CI [0.0004, 0.0005]), task significance (indirect effect = 0.0010, 95% CI [0.0007, 0.0012]), autonomy (indirect effect = 0.0017, 95% CI [0.0015, 0.0019]), feedback (indirect effect = 0.0010, 95% CI [0.0009, 0.0012]), and WFH feasibility (indirect effect = 0.0007, 95% CI [0.0006, 0.0008]) mediate the impact of COVID-19 on job salaries with 95% bootstrap CIs excluding 0. Thus, H3 is supported.

#### Conditional Indirect Effect of the COVID-19 Pandemic

To test the moderated mediation (conditional indirect) effect of occupational nonroutineness on job salaries, we used Monte Carlo bootstrapping to calculate the 95% CIs of the conditional indirect effects and their differences (Frieder et al., 2018). As the moderating effect of occupational nonroutineness on the relationship between COVID-19 and work design characteristics was supported, we directly tested the moderated mediation effect, as shown in Table 5. In terms of skill variety, the conditional indirect effect is weaker at high nonroutineness (indirect effect $ _{high} = -0.0039 $, 95% CI [-0.0042, -0.0036]) rather than low nonroutineness (indirect effect $ _{low} = 0.0014 $, 95% CI [0.0011, 0.0018]), and the difference between the two conditional indirect effects is significant (indirect effect $ _{diff} = -0.0053 $, 95% CI [-0.0059, -0.0048]). Thus, H4a is supported. Similarly, the conditional indirect effects are stronger for occupations with low nonroutineness than for those with high nonroutineness, and the differences are significant for task identity, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility, thus supporting H4. However, we do not observe a significant conditional indirect effect of COVID-19 on job salaries for occupations at different nonroutineness through task significance (indirect effect $ _{diff} = -0.0002 $, 95% CI [-0.0006, 0.0002]). Overall, the indirect effects of enriched work design characteristics on the relationship between COVID-19 and job salaries are attenuated for nonroutine occupations.

#### Robustness Checks

We performed several robustness checks to ensure the reliability and validity of our findings. For brevity, the results of these analyses are reported in Appendix E.

An alternative measure of treatment intensity. Instead of using the cumulative number of confirmed COVID-19 cases to measure treatment intensity, we used the annual number of new confirmed COVID-19 cases for each city each year. We then created an alternative measure, COVID New Case, which took a value of 0 before the pandemic, and after the pandemic, was calculated as the average ratio of annual new confirmed cases to the population of the city where the employing firm was located. All results presented in Appendix E, Tables E1–E4 are consistent with our main findings.

A composite score of work design characteristics. To capture the overall richness of work design, we computed a Motivating Potential Score (MPS) for each job posting, based on the Job Diagnostic Survey framework developed by Hackman and Oldham (1975). This measure integrates five core work design characteristics and was computed using equation (3). This composite measure reflects the theoretical assumption that jobs characterized by high levels of these characteristics foster greater internal motivation, job satisfaction, and performance. Using this composite score, we re-estimated our analyses, and the results presented in Appendix E, Table E5 further support our hypotheses.

 $$ M P S=\left(\frac{Skill Variety+Task Identity+Task Significance}{3}\right)\times Autonomy\times Feedback $$ 

#### Other Compensations

To deepen our understanding of organizational compensation strategies during the COVID-19 pandemic, we conducted additional analyses to examine how the pandemic influenced various forms of compensation and how occupational nonroutineness shaped these relationships. Specifically, we constructed binary variables to measure whether employers offered jobs with certain benefits, namely merit pay, stock options, training opportunities, subsidies, and insurance (Dulebohn et al., 2009). For example, Merit Pay was assigned a value of 1 if a job posting mentioned keywords related to merit pay, such as “merit pay,” “pay-for-performance,” “variable pay,” and “performance bonus / reward / allowance,” and 0 otherwise. A similar approach was used to measure Stock Options, Training, Subsidy, and Insurance, with lists of keywords provided in Appendix F, Table F1. Given the binary nature of these variables, we used probit regression to estimate the effects of COVID-19 on these various forms of compensation and the moderating effect of occupational nonroutineness on these relationships.

Results presented in Appendix F, Table F2, show significant changes in compensation strategies in response to the pandemic. Specifically, the pandemic was associated with a reduction in performance-related pay, such as merit pay (b = -0.022, p < .001, column 1), stock options (b = -0.034, p < .001, column 3), and training opportunities (b = -0.007, p < .001, column 5), likely due to financial constraints during the economic downturn. Conversely, organizations increased subsidies (b = 0.004, p < .05, column 7) after COVID-19, offering enhanced benefits for meals, transportation, and accommodations. Furthermore, we observe a significant negative effect of

COVID-19 on insurance benefits (b = -0.009, p < .001, column 9).

Occupational nonroutineness significantly moderated the relationship between COVID-19 and various forms of compensation. It mitigated the negative effects of COVID-19 on merit pay  $ (b = 0.009, p < .001 $, column 2), stock options  $ (b = 0.019, p < .001 $, column 4), and training opportunities  $ (b = 0.008, p < .001 $, column 6). In contrast, the positive effect on subsidy was amplified for nonroutine occupations  $ (b = 0.011, p < .001 $, column 8), whereas the negative effect on insurance was more pronounced  $ (b = -0.010, p < .001 $, column 10). These findings reveal how organizations adjusted compensation strategies to align with occupational contexts, highlighting adaptive responses to workforce challenges during the crisis.

## Discussion

This study investigates the cascading effects of COVID-19 on organizational work design and compensation strategies by using an advanced LLM-based approach to analyze large-scale text data from a leading Chinese online job posting platform. Our findings reveal that the pandemic enhanced the six work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. Notably, the positive effects of COVID-19 on these characteristics were moderated by occupational nonroutineness. Specifically, nonroutine occupations exhibited attenuated positive effects on skill variety, task identity, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility, but an amplified effect on task significance. Mediation analyses further indicate that enhanced work design characteristics mediated the positive relationship between the pandemic and job salaries, with the occupational nonroutineness moderating these indirect effects.

Additionally, our analysis highlights heterogeneous adjustments in compensation strategies: while merit pay, stock options, training opportunities, and insurance declined, subsidies for meals, transportation, and accommodations increased.

#### Theoretical Implications

Our research contributes to the literature on work design and compensation by deepening the understanding of how work design and compensation strategies evolve in the post-pandemic world. First, our study advances a dynamic perspective on work design research by empirically examining how large-scale external shocks, such as COVID-19, reshape work design. Addressing long-standing calls to incorporate contextual antecedents, such as technological changes, environmental shocks, and social interactions, into work design research (DeVaro, 2010; Parker & Grote, 2022; Wang et al., 2021), our empirical evidence demonstrates that work design is not static but adaptive in response to external shocks (Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017). This insight is particularly relevant in today's volatile environment, where organizations operate under increasing uncertainty that transcends national, economic, or societal boundaries (George et al., 2016), and where such major disruptions can leave lasting effects on organizational designs (Foss, 2021). Moreover, we add to work design theory by positioning WFH feasibility as an emergent and expanded work design characteristic that operates as an adaptive response, enabling organizations to maintain operational continuity and workforce adaptability amid disruptive external shocks (Parker, 2014; Wang et al., 2021).

Second, this study contributes to work design theory by highlighting the role of

occupational contexts as a key boundary condition shaping how organizations redesign and reward work in response to disruptive external shocks (Chauvin et al., 2024; Morgeson et al., 2010). While prior research on work design has largely treated work design characteristics as universal attributes or examined their differential effects on individual outcomes across occupations, less attention has been paid to how occupational contexts influence the evolution of work design (Dierdorff et al., 2025; Morgeson et al., 2010). Our findings bridge this gap by demonstrating that occupational nonroutineness moderates how organizations redesign work and adjust compensation in response to external shocks. Specifically, nonroutine occupations exhibited greater resilience to disruptions, showing less pronounced changes in work design. Conversely, routine occupations underwent more pronounced redesign effects, reflecting greater sensitivity to structural changes and increased task demands. Notably, task significance increased more for nonroutine occupations, suggesting the heightened salience of work meaningfulness in professional roles during crises. These results align with the notion of occupational resilience, which posits that individuals in cognitively demanding roles are better positioned to integrate trait hardiness, adaptive capacity, and iterative processes of appraisal and coping to navigate adversity (Kossek & Perrigino, 2016). By uncovering the occupation-specific patterns, our findings deepen understanding of how macro-level disruptive external shocks permeate occupational contexts to shape job-level work design characteristics and compensation strategies (Dierdorff et al., 2025; Morgeson et al., 2015; Parker & Knight, 2024).

Third, we enrich the compensation literature by integrating work design theory into the analysis of compensation strategies, particularly in the context of disruptive external shocks. The

COVID-19 pandemic, which compelled organizations to restructure both work design and reward systems, provides a valuable lens for advancing research on compensation management under crisis conditions. Our findings uncover nuanced shifts across multiple dimensions of compensation, extending beyond base salary to underexplored benefit components (Bapuji et al., 2020; Dulebohn et al., 2009). Specifically, organizations tended to respond by raising salaries linked to jobs with enriched work design characteristics, while reducing performance-related pay such as merit pay and stock options, curtailing training opportunities, and expanding welfare-based subsidies. These findings support a more integrative view of compensation, highlighting the dynamic interaction between internal work design and external disruptive shocks in shaping how organizations adjust their compensation strategies (Dulebohn & Werling, 2007).

Finally, our study introduces a novel methodological approach that employs LLMs to analyze textual data from over 1.8 million job postings. While traditional methods such as surveys and interviews provide valuable insights, they offer limited ability to capture dynamic changes and to establish causality across large, diverse samples (Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017; Wang et al., 2021). Moreover, self-reported measures are often subject to variability and subjective bias arising from individual perceptions and interpretations of job roles (Carlson & Burbano, 2025; Morgeson & Humphrey, 2006). The LLM-based approach addresses these limitations by offering a scalable, cost-effective, and objective alternative that enables fine-grained analyses of evolving work design trends across various domains, while allowing control for broad contextual factors, such as occupations, organizations, and industries (Chen et al., 2025; De Kok, 2025). This method also

outperforms traditional human coding in efficiency (Carlson & Burbano, 2025). Furthermore, insights derived from this approach and dataset extend beyond conventional occupational classification methods, offering a more nuanced understanding of both job-level demands and organizational-level design trends in the labor market (Chen et al., 2025). By showcasing the analytical potential of LLMs, our study demonstrates their transformative value in advancing HRM research.

#### Practical Implications

Our research offers several actionable insights for organizations seeking to adapt their work design and compensation strategies to the post-pandemic landscape. Our estimates indicate that a moderate increase in city-level COVID-19 exposure, such as five more cases per 1,000 residents, was associated with increases of 9–16% of a standard deviation across five core work design characteristics, 31% of a standard deviation in WFH feasibility, and 8% of a standard deviation in job salaries. These findings suggest that even moderate pandemic-related disruptions prompted substantial organizational adjustments in work design and compensation. They also highlight the importance of addressing macro-grand challenges to anticipate evolving work demands and guide evidence-based HR practices (George et al., 2016; Morgeson et al., 2015).

Furthermore, our findings shed light on the resilience and adaptability of modern work systems and underscore the need for organizations to adopt a dynamic perspective in their work design frameworks. Organizations should recognize that work design characteristics are inherently flexible and highly responsive to external shocks (Parker, Morgeson, et al., 2017). In particular,

considering occupation heterogeneity in evolving work design characteristics and compensation strategies may serve as an adaptive organizational response to future disruptions. This could involve offering competitive base salaries and expanded subsidy benefits to better support employees in nonroutine occupations during periods of instability. While our study does not directly evaluate the effectiveness of these actions, they may represent broader organizational efforts to navigate uncertainty and strengthen workforce resilience and adaptability.

Additionally, our study highlights the value of advanced LLMs for analyzing large-scale job-related data, such as job postings, employee resumes, and reviews. This scalable, cost-efficient approach enables organizations to identify workforce trends, perform detailed analyses of work design, and make data-driven decisions to optimize HR practices. Leveraging these tools can help organizations remain agile, adaptable, and prepared to face future challenges.

#### Limitations and Future Research Directions

Our study has several limitations that provide avenues for future research. First, the use of job posting data, while valuable for large-scale analysis, limits our ability to capture the detailed implementation processes, individual psychological responses, and occupational contextual variations underlying work design changes during the pandemic. For instance, although our job postings reflect formal role descriptions, they do not reveal nuanced details about the frequency or forms of feedback delivery (e.g., in-person conversations or digital check-ins). As a result, we are unable to examine how COVID-19 specifically reshaped feedback mechanisms in practice. Future research could address this gap through experimental or survey-based methods to explore these

subtler changes and the underlying explanatory mechanisms, such as managers' cognitive and motivational processes driving organizational adaptation. Additionally, while we introduce occupational nonroutineness to explore heterogeneity in pandemic-induced work redesign and compensation adjustments, this measure cannot fully capture the diverse, context-specific ways occupations adapted to these disruptions. Future studies could extend this line of inquiry by conducting occupation-specific analyses to uncover more nuanced and directional patterns of how organizations redesign work and adjust compensation in response to external shocks.

Second, our DID estimation captures pandemic-induced changes in work design and compensation, but does not assess their effectiveness or long-term impact. Due to the structure of our data, we were unable to further examine the effectiveness of these changes. Future research could evaluate their impact on employee satisfaction, team collaboration, and organizational productivity. In addition, although early evidence suggests that such changes may leave lasting effects throughout the organization (Foss, 2021), their implications for organizations and employees require deeper examination. For example, while WFH and hybrid work arrangements have become common across industries (Barrero et al., 2021), some firms are scaling back these practices through return-to-office mandates (e.g., Amazon, Dell, JP Morgan) (Westfall, 2025). This divergence raises important questions about the durability and effectiveness of these changes. Future research could address these issues by employing other methodologies, such as collecting survey data and archival data from employees and organizations, to track the work design and compensation changes and their impact on employees (e.g., engagement, well-being, performance),

teams (e.g., team cohesion, performance), and organizations (e.g., organizational culture, productivity). Moreover, our study focuses solely on WFH feasibility inferred from job descriptions and does not capture the actual WFH arrangement. The relationship between the disclosed feasibility of WFH in a job posting and its explicit designation as a WFH position may have evolved during the pandemic. Future studies could explore how these two dimensions interact and whether the observed shifts toward WFH arrangements represent enduring transformations or temporary crisis responses, particularly in light of evolving organizational policies, such as continuation, modification, or elimination of remote work practices.

Furthermore, although we provide empirical evidence of the effects of COVID-19 on several forms of compensation, the broader dynamics of post-pandemic compensation management, including its trends, antecedents, outcomes, and heterogeneity, need further investigation (Bapuji et al., 2020). Future research could incorporate macroeconomic contexts, such as labor shortages or labor market tightness, to examine how firm-level work design adjustments interact with broader labor market dynamics to influence compensation strategies. Such studies could also explore organizational responses in compensation adjustments, including benefit increases, wage freezes, bonus reductions, and their variations across occupations and industries during disruptive external shocks. Additionally, investigating other forms of compensation, such as work–family benefits, location-based pay adjustments, home office stipends, and technology allowances related to remote work, would enhance our understanding of how firms adapt their compensation strategies in a post-pandemic world.

Finally, while our study centers on the COVID-19 pandemic as a major disruptive event, its broader theoretical contribution lies in advancing our understanding of how macro-level disruptive external shocks, such as public health crises, economic recessions, or technological revolutions, act as critical catalysts in reshaping work structure and experience (George et al., 2016). To deepen this line of inquiry, future research could build on event system theory (Morgeson et al., 2015) to develop a more integrated framework that explains how organizational practices and work design dynamically adapt to exogenous shocks. Such efforts would help bridge the gap between management research and societal needs, thereby providing valuable insights into proactively navigating complex global challenges (George et al., 2016).

## Conclusion

By employing a generative LLM-based approach to quantify work design characteristics from a large-scale dataset of online job postings, this study addresses emerging challenges of work design and compensation management in the post-COVID-19 era. It is among the first to explore the interaction between work design and compensation strategies in the context of a large-scale, disruptive external shock, while explicitly accounting for occupational heterogeneity. Our findings advance both work design theory and event system theory by revealing how COVID-19 reshaped work design characteristics and how these changes translated into adaptive compensation adjustments. Moreover, by identifying the moderating role of occupational nonroutineness, we provide insights into how organizations align their HR practices with distinct occupational contexts in a dynamic labor market. Methodologically, this study demonstrates the potential of LLM-based

text analytics as a scalable and objective approach for HRM research. In conclusion, our study makes both theoretical and methodological contributions to understanding how organizations redesign and reward work in response to disruptive external shocks and suggests directions for future research on work design and compensation management.
