### Appendix A. Details of Data Collection

For our analysis, we combined several sources of data to formulate the final dataset for difference-in-differences (DID) analyses examining the impact of COVID-19 on work design and compensation strategies.

First, to capture work design characteristics and various forms of compensation, we collected large-scale online job postings from a leading Chinese online hiring platform using a web-scraping algorithm. The dataset provides detailed information for each job posting, including job title, job description, expected salary, required education and work experience, benefits, and employer information.

Second, to account for heterogeneity across job types, we classified each job posting by mapping its position title to the Dictionary of Occupational Titles (DOT). Developed by the U. S. Department of Labor¹, the DOT has been widely used in labor market research and offers a conceptually consistent and empirically tractable framework for categorizing diverse job types (Cain & Treiman, 1981; Peng & Eunni, 2011; Poletaev & Robinson, 2008). Following Ge et al. (2023), we performed the mapping using translated job titles and keywords related to skill requirements and functional categories. To ensure accuracy, the mappings were manually verified through cross-checks with domain experts. The resulting job type classifications are listed below: (a). Professional, technical, and managerial occupations, (b). Clerical and sales occupations, (c). Service occupations, (d). Agricultural, fishery, forestry, and related occupations, (e). Processing occupations, (f). Machine trades occupations, (g). Benchmark occupations, (h). Structural work occupations, and (i). Miscellaneous occupations.²

Using the same procedure as above (Ge et al., 2023), we matched the job posting data with the Occupational Information Network (O*NET) database to construct a continuous measure of Nonroutineness, following Acemoglu and Autor (2011). This measure was calculated based on occupational categories using the Work Activities and Work Context Importance scales in the O*NET database. $ ^{3} $ To illustrate occupational heterogeneity, Table A1 presents the nonroutineness score across the four major occupational groups categorized by Acemoglu and Autor (2011), showing that managerial, professional, and technical occupations are predominantly specialized in abstract, nonroutine cognitive tasks. Figure A1 further depicts the distributions of the nonroutineness score across seven major occupations, with management occupations exhibiting the highest nonroutineness scores, followed by science occupations and business and financial occupations.

Furthermore, to ensure consistency with firm-level performance data, we restricted our sample to publicly listed Chinese firms for which performance data was available from external data sources, such as the Chinese Securities Market and Accounting Research (CSMAR) database. The CSMAR database provides comprehensive financial data on listed firms and is widely used in management research (Ge et al., 2023; Liu & Marquis, 2024; Su et al., 2023). We matched employer names from the job posting platform with those in CSMAR, considering

their common suffixes such as “Inc.,” “co.,” “ltd.”, and previous names (Ge et al., 2023). This integration allowed us to incorporate detailed firm-level characteristics, including location, size, age, and financial performance, to control for firm-level heterogeneity. Furthermore, we incorporated regional economic indicators, such as GDP per capita and the unemployment rate, sourced from the CSMAR dataset and the China City Statistical Yearbook. These measures enabled us to capture broader economic and labor market conditions.

Finally, we obtained COVID-19 epidemiological data from DXY, a leading digital healthcare technology platform that provides real-time city-level updates on epidemic conditions across China (Hu et al., 2020). This data source has been widely used in prior studies on the effects of COVID-19 (Ge et al., 2023; Liu & Marquis, 2024). Using firm-level job posting data, we constructed a measure of city-level COVID-19 exposure by aggregating the daily number of confirmed cases in each city where listed firms were located between 2020 and 2022, yielding the cumulative number of confirmed cases. To account for the influence of city population on the number of confirmed cases (Min et al., 2021), we collected city population data from the 2022 China City Statistical Yearbook and integrated it with job posting data based on firm location.

To ensure the reliability of our large language model (LLM) analysis, we excluded job postings with descriptions shorter than 250 words and removed internship postings. Furthermore, job postings missing key information, such as education and experience requirements, compensation information, firm performance, or COVID-19 case data, were excluded. The final dataset comprises 1,836,062 job postings from 3,183 publicly listed firms in China between 2018 and 2022.

### Appendix B. Technical Details of LLM-based Ratings

The following text presents the prompt used to assess work design characteristics and WFH feasibility. The {text} block indicates where an example query and the job posting information are entered. The example is included to instruct the LLM model to learn the expected input and output formats.

You are a professional evaluator of work design characteristics in the field of human resource management.

Your task is to assess the five core dimensions of work design—Skill Variety (SV), Task Identity (TI), Task Significance (TS), Autonomy (AU), and Feedback (FD)—as well as the feasibility of remote work (WFH) for each job based on the provided job advertisement information. The definitions of the work design dimensions and remote work feasibility are as follows:

Based on the work design model proposed by human resources scholars such as Greg R. Oldham, J. Richard Hackman, Frederick P. Morgeson, and Stephen E. Humphrey, five core dimensions and their definitions are:

• Skill Variety (SV): The extent to which a job requires an individual to use a variety of different skills to complete the work. For example, a job requiring mastery and application of diverse skills to perform work demonstrates a high level of skill variety.

• Task Identity (TI): The degree to which a job involves a whole piece of work, the results of which can be easily identified. For example, the job involves completing a piece of work that has an obvious beginning and end, and the job provides an individual the chance to completely finish the pieces of work he/she begins.

• Task Significance (TS): The degree to which a job has a substantial impact on the lives or work of others, whether inside or outside the organization. For example, the results of one’s work are likely to significantly affect the lives of other people, the job itself is very significant and important in the broader scheme of things, and the job has a large impact on people outside the organization.

• Autonomy (AU): The extent to which a job allows freedom, independence, and discretion to (a) schedule work, (b) make decisions, and (c) choose the methods used to perform tasks:

(a) Work Scheduling Autonomy: The job allows an individual to make his/her own decisions about how to schedule the work and the order in which things are done on the job.

(b) Decision-Making Autonomy: The job gives an individual a chance to use his/her personal initiative or judgment in carrying out the work.

(c) Work Methods Autonomy: The job allows an individual to determine what methods to use to complete the work.

• Feedback (FD): The degree to which the job provides direct and clear information about the effectiveness of task performance and others in the organization provide information about his/her performance.

(a) Feedback from job: The work activities themselves provide direct and clear information about the effectiveness (e.g., quality and quantity) of one’s job performance;

(b) Feedback from others: Other people in the organization, such as managers and coworkers, provide information about the effectiveness (e.g., quality and quantity) of one's job performance.

Additionally, Jonathan I. Dingel and Brent Neiman (2020) evaluate whether a job can be done at home based on conditions in the Generalized Work Activities survey response. Specifically, if a job involves any of the following tasks or activities, it is classified as one that cannot be performed at home:

• Performing General Physical Activities is very important.

• Handling and Moving Objects is very important.

• Controlling Machines and Processes [not computers nor vehicles] is very important.

• Operating Vehicles, Mechanized Devices, or Equipment is very important.

• Performing for or Working Directly with the Public is very important.

• Repairing and Maintaining Mechanical Equipment is very important.

• Repairing and Maintaining Electronic Equipment is very important.

• Inspecting Equipment, Structures, or Materials is very important.

Additionally, if a job involves any of the following keywords or related terms, it is classified as one that can be performed at home: work from home, WFH, teleworkable, remote work, work remotely, flexible work location, hybrid working, and hybrid working from home.

In this context, you are required to score each job's five core job design dimensions and assess the feasibility of WFH for each job posting based on the job title and description. Specifically,

The five work design characteristics are evaluated on a 5-point scale from 1 to 5: 1 = negligible or absent; 2 = low level; 3 = moderate level; 4 = relatively high level; 5 = high level.

The feasibility of WFH is measured using a binary scale: 1 = WFH-eligible; 0 = WFH-ineligible.

{Example}

Please score the jobs' five core work design characteristics and the feasibility of WFH based on the job description provided:

Job ID: {job_id}, Title: {title}, Description: {desc}

Please remember that your task is to determine the scores for the five work design characteristic dimensions, which should be integers between 1 and 5, and assess whether the job can be performed at home using 0 or 1. If you find it impossible to evaluate based on the information provided, please respond with "Null." Your scores should reflect the judgment of a professional scholar in work design and remote work feasibility, considering your extensive expertise in human resources. Be thoughtful and provide distinct scores. Provide only the scores in your response, with no additional information.

Response format: [id, SV score, TI score, TS score, AU score, FD score, WFH].

An example query is incorporated into the prompt as follows.

Below is an example of a query and answer:

Query: Please score the five core work design characteristics and the feasibility of WFH based on the job description provided. Here is the job description: Job ID: 10491456, Title: CAN Diagnostic Test Engineer for Huanghai Automobile, Description: 1. Develop plans and design schemes for vehicle CAN bus systems based on project requirements. 2. Draft specifications and testing plans for CAN bus requirements. 3. Develop CAN bus protocols based on system requirements. 4. Write DFMEA for network designs and optimize the network. 5. Oversee the acceptance testing of the vehicle CAN bus network. 6. Monitor and resolve network-related issues. Job Requirements: 1. Bachelor's degree or above in electronics, electrical engineering, automotive engineering, or related fields, with over three years of experience in vehicle bus systems. 2. Proficiency in using CANoe, CANscope, CANStress, and other bus testing equipment. 3. Familiarity with automotive CAN bus protocols and standards such as ISO11898 and J1939. 4. Capability to independently complete component and integration communication testing for CAN bus systems.

Answer: 10491456, 5, 4, 5, 4, 3, .

### Appendix C. Convergent Validity Analysis of LLM-generated Measures

Carefully evaluating construct validity is essential when using LLMs to develop new measures (De Kok, 2025). To ensure convergent validity, we conducted a multi-step validation process that compared our LLM-generated measures with both expert-coded measures and established benchmark measures.

## 1. Comparison of LLM-generated Measures with Expert Ratings

We first conducted a validation study to establish convergent validity by comparing the LLM-derived scores with human expert-coded ratings. Specifically, 30 experts with academic backgrounds in management were invited to independently evaluate a random sample of 50 job postings, assigning scores to six work design characteristics: skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. Before the rating process, the research team provided a brief instructional guide detailing the construct definitions, rating criteria, and illustrative examples for the six characteristics (see Appendix C-4). This ensured a shared understanding and minimized interpretive variability among the experts. Each expert scored the job postings based on the corresponding descriptions and their domain knowledge of typical job functions. The rating process took approximately one hour, and each participant received a stipend of 100 RMB for their contribution. Among the 30 experts, 56.67% were aged 25-30 years and 56.67% were female. All held or were pursuing postgraduate degrees, and more than half had at least three years of research experience in the field of management, ensuring a solid foundation in relevant theory and research.

To evaluate the reliability of expert assessments, we calculated intra-class correlation coefficients (ICCs) for each work design characteristic, as shown in Table C1. Specifically, we used the intraclass correlations for a single-rater, two-way random-effects model, denoted as ICC(1) by McGraw and Wong (1996). The results indicate that ICC(1) values exceed commonly accepted thresholds for small (.01), medium (.10), and large (.25) effect sizes (Woehr et al., 2015), ranging from .33 to .57. Feedback shows the lowest ICC(1) (.33) while skill variety and WFH feasibility demonstrate the highest levels (.57 and .56, respectively). These values suggest strong consistency among individual raters. Moreover, the reliability of the aggregated expert scores, as indicated by ICC(2), is uniformly high, ranging from .94 to .98. These results indicate excellent reliability of the aggregated expert ratings across all characteristics, confirming that experts produced a highly stable and dependable measure.

We next compared the averaged expert-coded scores to the LLM-derived scores using both Pearson and Spearman correlations. Across all six work design characteristics, the LLM-generated measures show strong correlations with expert-coded measures. For example, skill variety shows the highest correlation (r = .84, p < .001). Task significance and WFH feasibility also exhibit strong correlations with LLM-derived scores (r = .77, p < .001; r = .75, p < .001, respectively), while task identity and autonomy yield moderately strong correlations (r = .69 and r = .66, respectively). Feedback also has a meaningful correlation (r = .56, p < .001). The Spearman correlations are consistent with the Pearson correlation, further supporting the validity of LLM ratings. These findings provide robust evidence of convergent validity, confirming that the LLM-generated assessments closely align with expert human ratings and enhancing confidence in the validity of the LLM-based approach for large-scale research on work design.

## 2. Comparison of LLM-generated Measures with Existing Benchmarks

Demonstrating a strong correlation between LLM-generated measures and established benchmarks provides compelling evidence of construct validity, suggesting that the LLM-generated measures meaningfully capture the intended constructs. To assess this validity, we compared the LLM-generated measure of WFH feasibility with the established WFH index developed by Dingel and Neiman (2020), referred to as WFH_DN.

Dingel and Neiman (2020) assess the feasibility of WFH for all U.S. occupations using data from two O*NET surveys: the Work Context Questionnaire and the Generalized Work Activities Questionnaire. Based on these data, each occupation is categorized as either feasible or infeasible for WFH (i.e., whether the job can be performed at home). This benchmark measure has been widely used in subsequent research (e.g., Forsythe et al., 2020; Ge et al., 2023; Ham et al., 2024; Hu et al., 2021). To compare their classification with our LLM-generated measure, we mapped each job posting in our dataset to its corresponding O*NET occupations and assigned the associated WFH_DN value.

To evaluate convergent validity, we assessed both the proportion of agreement and the statistical association between the LLM-derived WFH feasibility and the benchmark  $ WFH\_DN $. We found a 74.25% agreement between WFH and  $ WFH\_DN $, indicating substantial consistency. A chi-square test further confirmed a statistically significant association between the two measures, supporting strong alignment between the LLM-based measure and human-coded classifications.

Given that both measures are binary, we also employed probit and logit regression analyses to assess the strength of their association. As reported in Table C2, the baseline models in columns (1) and (2) yield statistically significant and positive relationships (b = 1.897 in the logit model and b = 1.167 in the probit model, both p < .001) without fixed effects. These results remain robust when controlling for year, industry, city, and job type fixed effects (columns (3) and (4)), providing further evidence for the reliability and construct validity of the LLM-based WFH feasibility measure.

Moreover, we constructed three continuous proxy measures for WFH capability using the WFH feasibility indexes. Following Ge et al. (2023), WFH capability was measured as the share of WFH job postings to the total number of job postings. The first measure captures the average WFH index at the career cluster level, with clusters grouped by O*NET based on work activities that require similar skills. The second measure reflects the average WFH index at the job family level, as defined by O*NET based on similar work requirements, skills, education, training, and credentials. The third measure assesses city-level WFH capability, calculated as the share of WFH job postings to the total number of job postings in the city where the employing firm is located.

To evaluate the consistency of our measure, we compared the average WFH indexes generated by the LLM with those derived from the classification developed by Dingel and Neiman (2020). The average WFH indexes at the career cluster, job family, and city level are strongly correlated  $ (r = .611, .718, $ and  $ .661, $ respectively;  $ p < .001) $, indicating substantial alignment between the two measures. In conclusion, although the two WFH indexes are based on different sources and classification methods, their high correlations reinforce the construct validity of the LLM-based WFH feasibility measure.

请根据岗位描述对该岗位的五个核心工作维度和居家办公的可行性进行评判，以下是岗位描述。请在你的回复中提供评分及简要解释。回复格式：[id, SV分数, TI分数, TS分数, AU分数, FD分数, WFH]+ 评分理由。

Job ID: 51104731

Title: 采购审计专员

Description:

【岗位职责】

1、负责集团及各分子公司的采购招投标合规审计工作，对招标全过程进行监督检查；

2、负责集团及各分子公司采购类的定期审计，包含供应商的来源可靠性、询比价的充分性、价格的合理性等，发现问题及时向领导汇报；

3、负责集团及各分子公司采购类的监察审计调查，包含采购过程中可能存在的舞弊行为；

4、负责公司采购作业相关内控及风险管理工作，协助建立系统化的内部控制体系；

5、完成领导交办的其他工作任务；

##### 【任职资格】

1、审计、采购等相关专业本科以上学历，具备同行业工作经历者优先；

2、具有五年以上采购审计或采购工作经验，熟悉采购业务流程及风险管控体系、招投标法律法规和政策规定、供应商管理流程等；

3、具有良好的组织协调能力、分析判断能力、语言与文字表达能力、能够独立跨部门沟通，推动审计问题整改；

4、严谨细致，有责任心，能适应高强度的工作压力，能够独立思考，善于团队合作。

##### 评分理由：

- 技能多样性（SV）: 4。该岗位涉及采购审计、合规检查、风险管理等多种技能，要求审计员具备分析、沟通和协调能力，能够运用多种技能来完成工作。

任务完整性（TI）：4。岗位职责明确，审计工作有清晰的开始和结束，审计员能够从头到尾完成整个审计过程，并能识别工作结果。

任务重要性（TS）:5。采购审计对公司及其分子公司的合规性和财务健康至关重要，审计结果可能对公司决策和运营产生重大影响，因此任务重要性评分较高。

• 自主性（AU）：4。该岗位要求审计员能够独立思考和判断，具备一定的决策自主权，能够在审计过程中自主安排工作和时间。

• 反馈（FD）：4。审计工作本身能够提供关于工作表现的反馈，审计员可以通过审计结果和领导的反馈来评估自己的工作表现。

居家办公可行性（WFH）：1。该岗位的工作内容主要依赖于文档审查和数据分析，且没有涉及现场操作或体力劳动，因此可以在居家办公环境中完成。

##### [51104731, 4, 4, 5, 4, 4, 1]

##### Rating Rationale:

## 1. Skill Variety (SV): 4

The role requires a combination of skills, including procurement auditing, compliance inspection, and risk management. It also demands strong analytical, communication, and coordination capabilities, demonstrating a high degree of skill diversity.

## 2. Task Identity (TI): 4

The responsibilities are clearly defined, with the audit process encompassing a structured beginning and end. The auditor is typically responsible for conducting the audit from initiation to completion and can identify and present clear outcomes.

## 3. Task Significance (TS): 5

Procurement audits are vital to the compliance and financial integrity of the company and its subsidiaries. The audit findings can significantly influence decision-making and operational efficiency, reflecting a high level of task importance.

## 4. Autonomy (AU): 4

The position requires independent thinking and professional judgment, granting the auditor a considerable degree of decision-making authority. The individual is expected to manage audit schedules and workflows with a high level of self-direction.

## 5. Feedback (FD): 4

The nature of audit work inherently provides feedback on performance through audit results and supervisory evaluations. This allows the auditor to assess the effectiveness of their work based on both outcome and managerial input.

## 6. Work-from-Home Feasibility (WFH): 1

The work primarily involves document review and data analysis and does not require on-site presence or physical tasks. Therefore, the position is compatible with a remote work arrangement.

### B. Prompt and Response in English

## 4. Instruction for Expert Ratings on Work Design Characteristics and WFH Feasibility

Dear Sir/Madam,

Thank you very much for participating in our research. This study aims to invite you to rate five core work design characteristics and the feasibility of work-from-home (WFH) based on job descriptions from a leading online job posting platform. Below, you will find definitions for each of the six work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility, to guide your assessments.



<table border="1" style="margin: auto; word-wrap: break-word;"><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Dimension</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Definition</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Skill (SV)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">The extent to which a job requires an individual to use a variety of different skills to complete the work. For example, a job that requires the mastery and application of a broad range of skills—encompassing both hard skills (e.g., programming, mechanical design) and soft skills (e.g., teamwork, communication, negotiation), specialized technical expertise, integration of diverse competencies, or interdisciplinary knowledge, reflects a high degree of skill variety.</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Task Identity (TI)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">The degree to which a job involves a whole piece of work (rather than fragmented or short-term tasks), the results of which can be easily identified. For example, the job involves completing a piece of work that has an obvious beginning and end, and the job provides an individual the chance to completely finish the pieces of work he/she begin, allowing the individual to see the outcome of their efforts. Higher task identity is reflected in roles where individuals lead or execute entire work processes rather than only handling segmented tasks.</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Task Significance (TS)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">The degree to which a job has a substantial impact on the lives or work of others, whether inside or outside the organization. For example, the outcomes of this job may have a significant impact on the lives of others—such as colleagues, clients, or consumers—or on the operations of businesses and society at large. In a broader context, the job itself carries significant inherent importance. This dimension also considers whether the job involves critical decision-making, direct value creation, or whether errors in performance could result in serious consequences for others.</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Autonomy (AU)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">The extent to which a job provides freedom, independence, and discretion in scheduling work, making decisions, and selecting the methods used to perform tasks. (a) Work Scheduling Autonomy: The job allows an individual to make his/her own decisions about how to schedule the work and the order in which tasks are completed. This dimension also considers whether the job requires adherence to fixed shift arrangements or offers flexibility in managing one's time. (b) Decision-Making Autonomy: The job allows an individual to use his/her personal initiative or judgment in making work-related decisions. This includes whether the role requires strict adherence to others' instructions or has freedom in determining the content and direction of one's work. (c) Work Methods Autonomy: The job allows an individual to decide how tasks are carried out, including the choice of tools, procedures, or workflow. This dimension also considers whether the work involves standardized routines or allows for creative and strategic thinking.</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Feedback (FD)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">The degree to which the job provides direct and clear information about the effectiveness of task performance and others in the organization provide information about his/her performance, including: (a) Feedback from job: The work activities themselves provide direct and clear information about the effectiveness (e.g., quality and quantity) of one's job performance;</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2">Feedback (FD)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">(b) Feedback from others: Other people in the organization (e.g., managers and coworkers) provide information about the effectiveness of one's job performance.</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Key aspects to consider include: directness (explicit or implicit feedback); frequency (rare, occasional, or regular); format (formal evaluations, meetings, systematic data, or informal conversations); clarity (vague or specific, clear, actionable, and helpful to guide behavior and improve performance).</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">Work-from-Home (WFH)</td><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Evaluate whether a job can be done at home based on work activity characteristics from the job descriptions:</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Not feasible for WFH if the job involves any of the following task characteristics: Performing general physical activities; Handling and moving objects; Controlling machines and processes [not computers nor vehicles]; Operating vehicles, mechanized devices, or equipment; Performing for or working directly with the public; Repairing and maintaining mechanical equipment; Repairing and maintaining electronic equipment; Inspecting equipment, structures, or materials.</td></tr><tr><td style="text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;">Feasible for WFH if the job involves any of the following task characteristics: (1) Spatial and temporal flexibility, allowing work to be conducted from various locations and at flexible hours; (2) Digital workflows, including the use of online tools, platforms, or virtual collaboration environments to perform tasks; (3) Performance evaluation based on task completion, rather than requiring physical presence or monitoring of attendance.</td></tr></table>




#### Rating Guidelines

Please evaluate each job posting by carefully reading its corresponding job description and scoring it according to the following criteria:

#### (1) Rating the Five Core Work Design Characteristics

Use a 5-point Likert scale to rate each of the five work characteristics, where: 1 = Very Low; 2 = Low; 3 = Moderate; 4 = High; 5 = Very High.

For example, regarding the “Feedback” dimension, the following scale applies:

• 1 = Very little or no feedback. Employees receive almost no information—either from the task itself or from others—about how well they are performing.

• 2 = Infrequent and unclear feedback. Feedback is sporadic, often dependent on a supervisor’s occasional remarks or reactive problem identification. It tends to be delayed and vague.

• 3 = Moderate feedback, but inconsistent and not always direct. Employees may receive some feedback from partial task outcomes or colleagues, but it is neither systematic nor regular.

• 4 = Frequent and clear feedback. Employees regularly receive direct and explicit performance information from systems, supervisors, or clients, which can be used to guide improvement.

• 5 = Consistent, direct, and detailed feedback. The job is inherently rich in feedback, allowing employees to understand their performance in real-time. Feedback is provided promptly through systems, users, or clients, and is specific enough to support adjustment and improvement.

#### (2) Rating WFH Feasibility

Use a binary scale (0 or 1) to assess whether the job can be performed at home or not:

• 1 = Yes, the job can be performed at home;

• 0 = No, the job is not feasible to perform at home.

#### Rating Example for Each Dimension (for Reference Only)

Below are illustrative examples for rating Skill Variety, Task Identity, Task Significance, Autonomy, Feedback, and WFH Feasibility. Each of the five core work design characteristics is assessed on a 5-point scale, while WFH feasibility is evaluated on a binary scale. These examples are intended to guide your understanding of how to apply the rating criteria. Please note that there are no right or wrong answers.

Responsible for maintaining accurate records of merchandise sales, conducting inventory checks, and reconciling accounts, completing all required sales reporting as specified;3. Handle the daily store operations, including incoming merchandise inspection, shelf stocking, display, restocking, returns, and loss prevention.</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>This role directly impacts sales performance and customer experience, serving as a frontline representative of the business. Its significance is relatively high due to its influence on consumer satisfaction and revenue generation. However, the position typically does not involve participation in core business decision-making processes.</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>4</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Finance Assistant (Xiangxue Pharmaceutical)</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>1. Responsible for departmental accounting, including inventory management, material classification, shipment processing, delivery documentation, invoicing, reconciliation, and payment follow-up;2. Responsible for supplier selection, evaluation, assessment, oversight, and management; monitor the implementation process of procurement plans, provide timely feedback, and archive supplier records and procurement documents;3. Complete other tasks assigned by department leadership;4. Strictly comply with confidentiality agreements and safeguard the company's interests.</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>This role is directly tied to the company's financial operations and risk management processes. It influences multiple functional units through procurement coordination, supplier oversight, and financial documentation, making it relatively important.</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>5</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Legal Affairs / Lawyer</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>1. Responsible for risk assessment and review of company contracts and other related documents;2. Assist various departments in mitigating legal risks and improving internal control mechanisms;3. Support the department in handling minor legal disputes;4. Take the lead in managing significant legal disputes and litigation cases.</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>This role involves core responsibilities in corporate legal affairs. Errors in execution may lead to severe legal consequences and financial losses. The tasks are critically important for safeguarding the organization's interests.</td></tr></table>

### Rating Example (for Reference Only)

Job Title: Front-End Development Engineer

#### Job Description:

1. Responsible for front-end code development, communicating with product managers and UI designers to translate product requirements into feasible technical solutions;

2. Responsible for converting design drafts from graphic designers into front-ends for both PC and mobile platforms as required;

3. Optimize interfaces technically, build complete interaction flows, and enhance user experience;

4. Participate in the testing of software systems and websites.

#### Qualifications:

1. Over 3 years of front-end development experience, proficient in core front-end technologies such as HTML, CSS, JavaScript, jQuery, and Bootstrap;

2. Skilled in at least one mainstream front-end framework such as React, AngularJS, Vue.js, or Backbone, with experience in developing at least two commercial web projects;

3. Proficient in front-end development and debugging tools, familiar with build tools such as Grunt, Gulp, and Webpack, etc.;

4. Expertise in at least one programming language (e.g., Python, Java, C/C++), and familiarity with at least one common backend framework (e.g., Node.js, Play, Django);

5. Experienced in using Git for collaborative development;

6. Strong documentation writing skills;

7. Excellent independent problem-solving ability, quick learning aptitude, and effective communication skills.

#### Rating and Rationale:

#### Skill Variety: 4

1. Requires mastery of multiple front-end technical skills;

2. Involves cross-functional knowledge of backend languages and frameworks;

3. Demands soft skills such as documentation writing, communication, and learning ability;

4. However, it does not involve broader domains such as marketing, operations, or management, indicating slightly limited skill breadth.

#### Task Identity: 4

1. Covers multiple phases of the front-end development lifecycle, approaching a full product development loop;

2. Responsible for the outcomes of the development work;

3. While the role involves collaboration with product, design, and testing teams, the individual leads the front-end development and optimization process, demonstrating moderate task identity.

#### Task Significance: 4

1. Has a direct impact on the final product and the value of digital solutions;

2. Failure in task execution would significantly affect user experience and product quality;

3. Plays a key intermediary role in cross-functional collaboration, though the impact of the task on the organization is not directly decisive.



<table border=1 style='margin: auto; word-wrap: break-word;'><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Autonomy: 4</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>1. Has substantial discretion over task execution methods, though collaboration in planning technical solutions introduces some constraints;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>2. Maintains decision-making authority over implementation paths, including framework selection and code optimization;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>3. Enjoys flexibility in combining and applying different technical stacks.</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Feedback: 4</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>1. Work outcomes are highly visual and immediately verifiable;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>2. Frequent and clear feedback is available from the user experience;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>3. Testing and debugging tools offer multidimensional feedback;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>4. Continuous feedback is provided by cross-functional team members;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>5. However, the role still relies on external feedback sources to a considerable degree.</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>WFH: 1</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>1. The tools used in the work and workflows are fully digital;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>2. Communication and documentation can be conducted online;</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>3. The outcome can be completed and delivered remotely.</td></tr></table>

Note. The above rating examples are provided for reference only; there are no right or wrong answers to the questionnaire items. Please rate based on the job description, your domain knowledge, and the conceptual definitions.

Once again, thank you very much for your participation! If you have any questions during the process, please feel free to contact the research team.

# Informed Consent Form

Thank you very much for your willingness to participate in this study!

To ensure your informed and voluntary participation, please carefully read the following information before beginning the questionnaire and confirm your consent to proceed.

## 1. Purpose of the Study

This study aims to evaluate the content of online job postings and rate five core work design characteristics—Skill Variety, Task Identity, Task Significance, Autonomy, and Feedback—as well as the feasibility of work-from-home (WFH) for each job posting.

The purpose is to compare ratings generated by the large language model (ChatGPT-4o-mini) with expert human ratings to assess the consistency and reliability of the LLM-based measures.

To ensure accurate and consistent assessments, please review the printed reading materials and attend the accompanying 20-minute presentation, which provides detailed definitions and rating criteria for six dimensions.

## 2. Estimated Time

The questionnaire is expected to take approximately 60 minutes to complete, though the actual duration may vary by individual. It includes 50 job postings sourced from a leading online job posting platform. To support concentration and reduce fatigue, a scheduled 5-minute break will be provided after the first 25 ratings.

## 3. Data Confidentiality

All data collected will be used exclusively for academic research purposes. Your responses will remain strictly confidential and will not be shared with any third parties or used for commercial purposes.

## 4. Participant Compensation

You will receive a reward of 100 RMB upon successful completion and submission of the questionnaire.

## 5. Your Responsibilities

Please read each job description carefully and provide honest, thoughtful responses based on your expertise.

## 6. Voluntary Participation and Informed Consent

By proceeding with this questionnaire, you acknowledge that you have read and understood this consent form and voluntarily agree to participate in this study. If you choose not to participate, you may exit at any time.

Please read each job description carefully and evaluate the work characteristics of each job posting based on the information provided.

#### (1) Work design characteristics using a 5-point Likert scale:

1 = Very Low, 2 = Low, 3 = Moderate, 4 = High, and 5 = Very High.

(2) WFH feasibility using a binary scale:

1 = The job can be performed at home, and 0 = The job cannot be performed at home.

Please rate the following postings based on the job descriptions, your domain knowledge, and the conceptual definitions of work design characteristics and the feasibility of WFH.

1. Primarily responsible for the creative conception and direction of videos (mainly in English); conduct consumer insights based on project positioning to produce high-quality video concepts;

2. Take the lead in pre-production planning by defining video style, writing scripts, and designing storyboards with a strong emphasis on innovation

3. Develop the core video “big idea” aligned with product positioning and formulate video strategies tailored to distribution channels;

4. Manage and mentor the creative team, guiding members in ideation and empowering the team.

1. Passionate about advertising, film, music videos, and other video formats; highly imaginative with the ability to capture trending topics and generate creative ideas;

2. Experience working in advertising agencies preferred, with a proven record of commercially successful projects; overseas work experience is a plus;

3. Creative thinker with strong communication skills and a deep understanding of brand marketing concepts;

4. Deep appreciation for film and music, with keen insight into international brand advertisements and promotional videos; able to identify key highlights, excel in imitation and creative adaptation, familiar with cinematographic language, and skilled in multiple expressive techniques and video styles;

5. Experience independently leading projects is preferred;

6. Recommendations of previously collaborated teams (directors, cinematographers, editors, art directors, 3D motion designers) are also welcome.

### Appendix D. Parallel Trend Assumption Test

The parallel trend assumption is a critical prerequisite for ensuring the validity of causal inferences in difference-in-differences (DID) analyses examining the effect of COVID-19 on work design characteristics. To test this assumption, we estimated a dynamic DID model, following Beck et al. (2010) and Huang et al. (2024). This model includes a series of year-specific dummy variables interacted with the treatment intensity indicator, allowing us to trace the year-by-year differences in work design characteristics across jobs with varying levels of treatment intensity. The model specification is shown in Equation (1):

 $$ y_{i n t}=a+\sum_{\tau}b_{\tau}C O V I D I n t e n s i t y_{n}\times D_{t+\tau}+\sum_{k}c_{k}C o n t r o l s_{i n t}^{k}+Y e a r_{t}+I n d u s t r y_{n}+P r o v i n c e_{n}+J o b t y p e_{i}+\varepsilon_{i n t}\quad(1) $$ 

where $t$, $n$, and $t$ respectively index job postings, employing firms, and years, respectively, and $y_{int}$ refers to the work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. COVID Intensity$_n$ is a treatment intensity indicator, measured as the cumulative confirmed COVID-19 cases divided by the city population in 2022. $D_{t+\tau}$ is a binary and set to 1 if year $t$ is $\tau$ years away from the outbreak of COVID-19 in 2020. We controlled for year, industry, province, and job type fixed effects.$^4$

As shown in Figure D1 (a) – (f), we found that there were no statistically significant differences in trends between the treated and controlled groups in the years prior to the pandemic for the six work design characteristics, supporting the parallel pre-trends assumption. After the COVID-19 outbreak, we observed a statistically significant difference beginning in 2020, and the coefficients gradually increased, indicating that the pandemic effectively prompted firms to increase the requirements for skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility. These results suggest that the parallel pre-trend assumption holds.

<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Figure D1. Dynamic Effects of COVID-19 on Work Design Characteristics</div> </div>


Note. This figure shows the dynamic effects of COVID-19 on work design characteristics before and after the pandemic from 2018-2022. The points represent the coefficients obtained from equation (1). Robust standard errors are used, and the dashed lines extending from the points represent 95% confidence intervals.

### Appendix E. Robustness Checks

## 1. Alternative Measure of Treatment Intensity

We introduced an alternative measure of treatment intensity to further support our main findings. Specifically, we used COVID New Case as an alternative measure of COVID Case, measured as the average share of annual new confirmed COVID-19 cases to the population of the city where the employing firm is located. This measure was applied only during the post-COVID-19 period and assigned a value of 0 for the pre-pandemic period. The regression results using this alternative measure are shown in Tables E1–E4. Consistent with our baseline findings, the results remain robust and continue to support our proposed hypotheses.

## 2. Composite Score of Five Work Design Characteristics: The Motivating Potential Score (MPS)

As a robustness check, we used a composite score of work design characteristics—the Motivating Potential Score (MPS)—to capture the overall richness and motivational features of job design, instead of using discrete, ordinal measures. The MPS integrates five core work design characteristics, including skill variety, task identity, task significance, autonomy, and feedback, based on the Job Diagnostic Survey framework developed by Hackman and Oldham (1975). This metric is grounded in the theoretical assumption that jobs scoring highly on these dimensions are more likely to foster internal motivation, job satisfaction, and improved performance. To operationalize MPS as a continuous variable, we followed the standard formula: the average of skill variety, task identity, and task significance was multiplied by autonomy and feedback, following Hackman and Oldham (1975). Using this composite score as the dependent variable, we re-estimated the effect of the COVID-19 pandemic on work design characteristics.

The results in column (1) in Table E5 are consistent with our baseline results, further reinforcing the finding that COVID-19 enhanced the core work design characteristics (b = 0.020, p < .001). The coefficient of the interaction term of COVID Case × Nonroutineness is significant and negative (b = -0.007, p < .001), indicating that the positive impact of COVID-19 on motivating potential score is attenuated for nonroutine occupations. Additionally, according to MacKinnon et al. (2002), the unreported indirect effect via MPS is 0.0020 with a 95% bias-corrected CI of [0.0018, 0.0023], indicating that the indirect effect is significant and that MPS partially mediates the relationship between COVID-19 and job salaries. The unreported results of the moderated mediation analysis using MPS as a mediator show that the conditional indirect effect is weaker at high (indirect effect $ _{high} $ = 0.0010, 95% CI [0.0008, 0.0014]) rather than low (indirect effect $ _{low} $ = 0.0028, 95% CI [0.0025, 0.0030]) level of nonroutineness and the difference (indirect effect $ _{diff} $ = -0.0017, 95% CI [-0.0020, -0.0013]) of the two conditional indirect effects is significant, thereby supporting H4.

## 3. Robustness Checks without Applying Winsorization

To assess the robustness of our findings to the winsorization procedure, we re-estimated our models without applying the winsorization procedure to the continuous variables. As shown in Tables E6-E9, all results are consistent with our baseline findings, further supporting our hypotheses. Specifically, Table E6 reports the regression results examining the impact of COVID-19 on the six work design characteristics. All coefficients of COVID Case are positive and statistically significant at the 0.1% level, providing strong support for H1.

Table E7 shows the regression results for the moderating effect of occupational nonroutineness on the relationship between COVID-19 and the six work design characteristics, using specifications without winsorizing the continuous variables. In column (1), the interaction term COVID Case × Nonroutineness is negative and statistically significant for skill variety (b = -0.016, p < 0.001), suggesting that the positive effect of COVID-19 on skill variety is attenuated for occupations with higher nonroutineness. Similar negative moderating effects are shown in columns (2), (4), (5), and (6) on task identity, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility, respectively. However, in columns (3), the coefficient of COVID Case × Nonroutineness for task significance (b = 0.007, p < 0.001) is significant and positive, indicating that the positive impact of COVID-19 on task significance is strengthened for nonroutine occupations. Overall, the findings remain consistent, further supporting the robustness of our main finding.

<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Table E7. The Moderating Effect of Nonroutineness on the Relationship between COVID-19 and Work Design Characteristics without Applying Winsorization</div> </div>

Table E8 reports the mediation analysis examining whether work design characteristics mediate the relationship between COVID-19 and job salaries, using models without winsorizing procedures. The 95% bootstrap confidence intervals for the indirect effects of all six work design characteristics exclude zero, indicating statistically significant partial mediation. These findings provide further support for H3.

Table E9 presents the conditional indirect effects of occupational nonroutineness on the relationship between COVID-19 and job salaries, without winsorizing continuous variables. The conditional indirect effects are significantly attenuated for nonroutine occupations through skill variety, task identity, autonomy, feedback, and WFH feasibility, with 95% confidence intervals for the differences between the conditional indirect effects at low and high nonroutineness excluding zero. However, the conditional indirect effect through task significance is not significant, as its confidence interval for the difference includes zero. Overall, these results are consistent with our main findings and further underscore the robustness.

<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Table E8. Mediating Effect of Work Design Characteristics on the Relationship between COVID-19 and Salary without Applying Winsorization</div> </div>

### Appendix F. Additional Analysis

To deepen our understanding of organizational compensation strategies in the context of COVID-19, we conducted additional analyses to examine how the pandemic influenced various forms of compensation and how occupational nonroutineness shaped these relationships. We used the following keywords in Chinese to identify different forms of compensation, including merit pay, stock options, training opportunities, subsidies, and insurance, from job descriptions and welfare information. Table F1 lists the related keywords in English.

word-wrap: break-word;'>Merit Pay</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Merit pay, pay-for-performance, variable pay, performance bonus, performance rewards, performance allowance, performance incentive, commission-based payment, monthly / quarterly / yearly / annual / half-year / bi-monthly / year-end bonus, performance profit sharing, overachievement bonus, performance-based commission, performance-based salary, performance-based pay, performance-related pay, incentive-based commission, project bonus, high rebates, variable commission, target achievement bonus</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Insurance</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Pension / retirement / endowment / health / medical / maternity / childbirth / commercial / commercial accident / accident / accidental injury / work injury / critical illness / major disease / severe illness / aviation accident / air travel accident / accident insurance</td></tr></table>

We examined the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on various forms of compensation, with regression results presented in Table F2. The analyses indicate that the pandemic led to a significant reduction in performance-based pay, including merit pay (b = -0.022, p < .001), stock options (b = -0.034, p < .001), and training opportunities (b = -0.007, p < .001). In contrast, organizations expanded employee subsidies (b = 0.004, p < .05), offering enhanced meal, transportation, and accommodations benefits, but reduced employee insurance (b = -0.009, p < .001). These findings reveal that organizations adjusted their compensation strategies to manage financial constraints while supporting employee well-being and fostering workforce resilience during the crisis.

Furthermore, we investigated the moderating role of occupational nonroutineness in the relationship between COVID-19 and compensation. As shown in columns (2), (4), and (6) of Table F2, the negative effects of the pandemic on performance-related pay were mitigated, especially for merit pay (b = 0.009, p < .001), stock options (b = 0.019, p < .001), and training opportunities (b = 0.008, p < .001). Furthermore, the positive effect of COVID-19 on subsidies was amplified for nonroutine occupations (b = 0.011, p < .001) whereas the negative effect on insurance was more pronounced (b = -0.010, p < .001). Overall, these results underscore the nuanced ways in which COVID-19 affected organizational compensation strategies across occupations, highlighting the importance of reassessing benefit structures for employees in professional roles.
